On the seventh day she broke one, and it became Garuḍa, who bowed to his mother and said, "Whatever you need done, I shall do." She said, "Because of the nāga's wrongdoing, destroy the nāgas!" From then on Garuḍa eats the nāgas.

He is also called Khageśvara, "Lord of Sky-Goers."

Viṣṇuratha, "Viṣṇu's Chariot": the eggs of a ṭiṭṭibha bird that lay on the shore of the ocean were carried into the sea by the waves. The bird, wishing to drain the ocean dry, kept dipping its wings in the water and drawing them out. A sage remarked, "Animals have little power." Garuḍa, who dwelt there, heard this and grew angry; he covered the ocean with half his wings and raised the other half into the sky. The nāgas dwelling there were terrified, bowed down, and at once gave the eggs back. Then the gods too became greatly afraid, and Viṣṇu emanated as a very emaciated sage and went before him. Garuḍa felt compassion for him and stayed. The sage said, "I have a task of great importance, but I cannot carry it out with this body; you accomplish it." Garuḍa agreed, saying, "I shall do it." At that he fastened him by the nose and made him steadfast. Then he said, "Carry me to the land of the gods." Here his becoming Viṣṇu's mount is told.

Nāgāntaka, "Ender of Nāgas," is the one who destroys them. The Golden-Winged, because his wings are beautiful like the color of gold. Pannagāśana, "Eater of Belly-Crawlers," eats snakes. These are the names of Garuḍa.

Now, beginning with Īśāna, the gods of the directions and so on will be explained. With five verses come the synonyms for the name of Īśvara. Śam is an indeclinable meaning good. Because he possesses it, he is also Possessed of Bliss. Śambhu, "Source of Bliss," because he always abides in a form of supreme joy: by the rule "śaṅku and the rest," ku and the elision of the final. By the rule "sus and sup have one meaning," a compound. By the rule "because desire arises fully, the bhūtas," there are also īśa, īśvara and īśāna for the Lord, the Mighty One and the Master. From "near the end in ak," the affix ka; and for īśvara, by the particle "in its own sense," also vara. By the rule "in capacity, age and disposition," śāna optionally.

Paśupati, "Lord of Beasts": the master Rabhasa says he is the lord because, just as goats, deer and the like are tied with a rope, he too has bound his retinue, the Pramathas and the rest. Others say that the beasts are persons, and because he rescues them from the bonds of saṃsāra he is the lord of the field.

Śiva, "the Benign," has become unchanging, because his nature is supreme joy, free from the states of the qualities: by the rule "śiva and the rest," kvan, the change to i, and the elision of m.

His weapon is the spear, so he is Śūlin.

Maheśvara (Śiva), "the Great Lord," because there is no other king above him.

Śarva, "the Destroyer," because at the destruction of the eon he destroys everything. Śṛṇoti is in the sense of the destruction of beings: from gṛ and śṛ, the affix va.

Śaṅkara: śam is joy that has the character of the highest good; because he brings it about, he is Śaṅkara, "Maker of Bliss": for karoti, the affix ac.

Candraśekhara, "Moon-Crested": the moon that arose from the churning of the ocean is his crown ornament.

Bhūteśa, "Lord of Beings," because by means of the five elements, earth and the rest, he generates people in due order and destroys them utterly, as it says in the Ratnakośa. Or else he is the lord of the bhūtas through the Pramathas and the rest.

Khaṇḍaparaśu, "Broken Axe": it is told that in time of battle Īśvara's axe had its edge chipped.

Girīśa, "Lord of the Mountain," is the lord of Mount Kailāsa.

Giriśa: when his retinue, the Pramathas and the rest, were enjoying themselves on Himālaya, the snowy mountain, he by emanation made the form of the mountain small. Since there is no pra and the like, the affix ka. Or else he lies and dwells on the mountain: the affix ḍa.

Mṛḍa, "the Gladdener," makes beings happy because the qualities of sattva are complete in him: from "near the end in ik," the affix ka.

Mṛtyuñjaya, "Conqueror of Death": the signs of death appeared to the sage called Śveta, "the White," and by reciting the text Mṛtyuñjaya, blessed by Īśvara, he conquered the signs of death; hence the Conqueror of Death. By the rule beginning "bhṛ, vṛ, tṛ," the affix kha.

Kṛttivāsas, "Clad in Hide": his garment is made from the skin of a leopard.

Pinākin: his bow is called Pināka, the name of the bow of Īśvara alone.

The Pramathas are Īśvara's retinue, so he is Pramathādhipa, "Lord of the Pramathas."

Ugra, "the Fierce," because when he is angry he thinks, "There is no one above me." Un is in the sense of gathering. By the rule "jila, dūra and the rest," the affix sarka, and its being so too.

Śrīkaṇṭha, "Glorious Throat": from the churning of the ocean there arose a vessel full of heaped black poison. If it were set down anywhere else it would kill beings, and if it went into the belly it would burn, so Īśvara placed it in his throat. By its power his throat became exceedingly beautiful, like the color of beryl; hence the Glorious Throat. It is a compound referring to another thing, just as in "my mouth is Sarasvatī and my throat is Śrī." For that very reason he is also called Śitikaṇṭha, "Black-Necked." He is also Kapālabhṛt, "Skull-Bearer."

Vāmadeva, "God of the Left": as a lover is beautiful with sidelong glances, so, because he abides beyond the world, he has turned away from the world, or else because he is exceedingly excellent, he is the God of the Left.

Mahādeva, the Great God, because he is worshipped even by all the gods.

He whom all the gods
Worship with worship great in measure:
"Mahā" is the root that means "to worship";
Therefore the Great God is called "the Greatly Worshipped."

So it says in the Sāsbrasuraṇa.

"Varied Eyes" occurs as vitupākṣa: his right eye is the sun, his left the moon, and in the middle is the eye of fire. By the rule "after sakthi and akṣi, when it is a part of one's own body," the suffix ṣac applies. He is also called the Three-Eyed (Trilocana). "Fire-Seed" will be explained in the section on Kumāra, son of the Pleiades. Of ancient lineage, the Trunk. He is also called the All-Knowing (Sarvajña) and the Bearer of a Load of Matted Locks (Dhūrjaṭi): the root jaṭa is in the sense of massing together. By "ir," there is the a of the uṇādi class. The load is a mass: his mass of matted locks.

When Brahmā performed a fire offering, dust fell from his forehead into the fire, and the fire turned blue and red. Since Īśvara was born from it, he is called the Blue-Red (Nīlalohita). Thus it is said:

The horse came forth red,
And then turned blue once more;
From these, called blue and red,
This Lord came forth.

The Seizer (Hara) is so called because by the quality of darkness he seizes all beings; the suffix is ac. Smara is the god of desire. Because he burned him by sending forth fire from his eye, he is the Destroyer of Smara (Smarahara). Because at the time of the destruction of the eon he destroys all beings, he is the Destroyer (Bharga). In the sense of the agent too, the suffix is ghañ. Of bhrastha, bharja. Ambaka means eye. Because he burned the triple-tiered city of the asuras with the fire of his arrow, he is the Ender of the Three Cities (Tripurāntaka). The Bearer of the Gaṅgā (Gaṅgādhara): at the time when all the abodes of the heavens were burned by the fire of the eon, when the goddess Gaṅgā fell and came down, he stretched out his locks in the sky and held her upon his locks. Because of the way the word is formed, the Three-Eyed is mentioned here again: the earlier one was trilocana, while here tri and ambaka are joined, giving tryambaka. From then on he was known as the Bearer of the Gaṅgā.

Because he slew the asura called the Blind (Andhaka), he is the Enemy of Andhaka (Andhakaripu). The Bull-Bannered (Vṛṣadhvaja) is the one with the ox upon his banner. The Destroyer of the Sacrifice (Kratudhvaṃsin): Umā's father, the sage called the Clear One, invited all the gods in order to perform a sacrifice, but because Īśvara went about naked, he thought it shameful and did not invite him. Thereupon, it is said, Īśvara grew angry and killed him. The Sky-Haired (Vyomakeśa) is the one whose locks are stretched out across the sky: it is a compound with the seventh case, in the sense of pervasion and of locus. Because its meaning is already conveyed, no verb is supplied.

Becoming (Bhava): since his qualities are complete and the manifold arises from him, he is Becoming. From bhū, which replaces asti, by the rule on ṛt and ut, the suffix asa. By the rule "after śri and bhū, when there is no pra or the like," the suffix ghañ applies to bhū and the others that go together with śri. The Terrible (Bhīma): because he takes the form of the Great Terrifier (Mahābhairava) and makes the three realms tremble, he is the Terrible; by the rule "from bhī, also ṣuk," with ma, bhīmaka. Bhava, the Abider (Sthāṇu), is the one who remains even when the eon is destroyed; by the rule "dhenu and the like," there is also the ṇa of sanu. By "of ru," the r is elided, and when dra is removed, by "from aṇ," the vowel is lengthened.

The Weeper is Rudra: when Brahmā made Īśvara take birth, Īśvara then wept, and so he became known as the Weeper. Thus it is said:

You obtained the name Rudra
Because you wept.

From tañci, vañci, śaki, kṣipi, kṣudi, tudi, madi, mandi and canda, the suffix raka.

Umā: when Īśvara said, "I am going to the Snowy Mountain (Himavat) to practice austerities," Umā stiffened and said "U!" So she became known as Umā; elsewhere she is known as the Fierce One. Her lord is Īśvara (Umāpati). These are the names of Īśvara. It is a name formed in the manner of tudra.

"Filling with Water and Purifying" is the name of Īśvara's own matted locks; it occurs as kasarda. Sarbba is in the sense of completion. By the rule "sampad and the like," kvip. The v after r is elided. Ka refers to the water of the Gaṅgā, and pūra is filling; because it makes a sound and purifies, by the rule "from sus," ka. Or else ka is that which has become an ornament, namely Brahmā's head; for that in which it is held, the suffix ḍa: kasarddaḥ. It is also called the Bound Locks (jaṭājūṭa).

"Pervading the Sorrowless" is the name of Īśvara's bow: because the mass of blazing fire from the arrow that burned the three cities pervaded the sky and destroyed them, it is pināka. That asi is here explained as asihita expresses the action; it expresses a compound of the pra class. Because it belongs to the śakantu group, the letter a takes the form of what follows. Its synonym is the Sounding Bow: by the rule "from gāṇḍi and ajaga, in a name," the suffix va, and by the rule "in others too, the Unborn Pervading," the final of the stem is long. Pūrṇacandra gives ajagāvaṃ as an example under the sūtra on keśa and the like. In the Garland of Names (Nāmamālā), the first vowel appears long: "sthaṇardhanurajagāvaṃ." Śaṅga, dhanu, smarāri, ajagāvaṃ, ajasya bāpaikaṃ: these are explained in the Ratnakośa, and also by Puruṣottama in the commentary on the Bhāṣā. Others say that in the rule "from gaṇḍi and ajaka, in a name," aja is Viṣṇu and ka is Brahmā, and because it is endowed with them, va.

"His retinue is the Pramathas and the rest": here sāriṣa, "retinue," is formed as sāriṣada. These are the hosts who crush Īśvara's adversaries: Caṇḍa, the Delighter Lord (Nandīśvara), Mahākāla, Bhṛṅgiṇi and the rest. From sāriṣada, by the rule "arghyaśca," ṇa. They are explained as the retinue of Pramathas: because they utterly crush those hostile to Rudra, the suffix ac.

"Brahmāṇī and the other mothers (mātṛkās)": when Īśvara fought with the asura Tutu, at that time Īśvara's dust fell to the ground, and from it arose the seven mothers, Brahmāṇī and the rest, namely Brahmāṇī, Vaiṣṇavī, Aindrī, Vārāhī, Kauberī, Raudrī and Kaumārī. Brahmāṇī is spoken of as a woman of the royal caste; under the rule "after one ending in ṇi, in distinction, before aṇ," master Puruṣottama made it both the thing illustrated and the illustration. By the rule "after a prior word, in a name," it has ṇa. By "ṭit, ḍha, aṇ and the like," dīpa. Thus:

She who arose from Brahmā's power
Is designated Brahmāṇī.

So it is said; she is the great-souled Caṇḍī. Where "astu" is read in "Brahmāṇī and the rest," the meaning is that this, belonging to Brahmā, is Brahmāṇī: by the rule "from the remainder," aṇ. By the rule "not in the uṇādi formations," the ac and the like of anti are elided.

"She who became Brahmāṇī" is the power of Brahmā, and Bhāratī, and the one called Somavallarī: so says Rudra too. And master Abhinanda says it too, in the Bālarāmāyaṇa:

O Brahmāṇī, Kauberī,
Vaiṣṇavī, Raudrī, Withering One,
Caṇḍī, Moon Goddess, to you I bow.

Pervasive Lordship is being lord of all excellences. Its synonym is the Pervading. Minuteness and the rest are the eight qualities of lordship: minuteness, lightness, attainment, full attainment, likewise worthiness of worship, mastery, control, and abiding as one wishes. By this division into subtlety and the rest, it is designated the eightfold wealth of the qualities of lordship.

Of these, subtlety: because one whose qualities have become subtle enjoys the objects of enjoyment together with their causes and results, it is called subtlety. Lightness is that very lightness by which one is exceedingly light, like the wind. Attainment is fully attaining whatever one wishes, for example touching the moon even with the tip of a finger. Full attainment is having no obstacle to one's wishes, for example diving into the earth just as into water. Worthiness of worship is being worshipped itself: being praised by one's retinue. Mastery is being sovereign: transforming moving and unmoving beings. Control is subjugating: acting upon one's own continuum. One whose disposition is to abide by desire in what is desired; the state of such a one is abiding as one wishes. Because roots have many meanings, the root śīṅ here has the sense of abiding: the meaning is the quality of abiding everywhere, on the earth, in the sky and in the intermediate space. These are distinguished by master Jayamaṅgala in his extensive commentary on the Seventy.

A verse and a half give the names of Umā. Umā means "mā, mā," "do not, do not": because she was forbidden from austerities, she was thenceforth known as Umā, and she goes as the Fair-Faced One. Because she is the daughter of the one called Katya, she is Kātyāyanī, Daughter of Katya. By the rule "garga and the like," yañ. By the rule "for lohita and the like, and for those ending in śakala," ṣpha. Because of ṣit, dīṣa.

The Fair One (Gaurī): Īśvara entered upon austerities and said, "Fair One, I am practicing an austerity of not dwelling together with you." In the first month of summer, as she was scorched by four fires from the four directions and by the sun from above, Īśvara came, drew the Gaṅgā up from beneath the earth, and quenched the fires. At that her complexion turned dark, and when he called her "Dark One," she grew angry again and underwent hardship, and her complexion turned fair, so it is said. By the rule "gaurī and the like," dīṣa; and by "bhaja, goṇa and the like," dīṣa. According to the difference of circumstance, she is called the White One and the Dark One.

The Daughter of the Snowy Mountain (Haimavatī): since she is the daughter of the god of the snow mountains, she is the Daughter of the Snowy Mountain; by the rule "in the sense of descendant," aṇ. By the rule "from those of desire that have gone forth," as before, varac. By the rule "sībara and the like," ṭāraci; and the one called Īśvarī is also her name. Yābabī, karbarī, īśvarī and īśvarā are in the Trikāṇḍaśeṣa. Since power and the possessor of power are inseparable, the power of Śiva is Śivā. By the rule "indra, varuṇa and the like," Bhavānī, Śarvāṇī, Rudrāṇī and Mṛḍānī take dīṣa with an augment, in the sense of relation to a male.

Since all auspiciousness is in her and she makes it manifest, she is Auspicious to All (Sarvamaṅgalā). The Leafless (Aparṇā): because when she dwelt in austerity she did not consume even the old leaves of the trees. Mountain-Born (Pārvatī) is the daughter of the mountain; by the rule "in the remaining sense," aṇ. Dweller in the Fortress (Durgā): she abides in austerity on a mountain hard to approach; by "arśas and the like," ac. Caṇḍī is endowed with a fierce mood; the suffix ac. Or else, because it belongs to the śvana group, dīṣa. Caṇḍī is the Fierce-Acting One. By "yava and the like," kan. Before ka, uṇ is shortened. She is also called the Mother of Beings: here ambikā means mother. Of these, "the Dark One" and "the Fair One" are from the Trikāṇḍaśeṣa.

U is Śaṅkara: since he dwells together with her, it is a compound in the sense of another accompanied. Gaurī: under "in the sense of speech," for the word go, where "not for those with uṇādi suffixes" stands separately, there is the ḍo that is not uṇādi. Since the final of the suffix is indicated, dyau and rau do not apply here. "Some do not pronounce the letter g," explains master Vāmana, author of the commentary on Pāṇini. "Ot aut" occurs in the sūtras of Śākaṭāyana as well. If one asks why, even on Śākaṭāyana's system, there is no au here: because when it is explained as bearing the defining characteristic, it is "he citrago"; "he citragava" is not the form here. Master Rakṣita too says, "Because it is not stated in the explanation, there is no au here," and this second view is the correct one. Likewise in the Sheaf of Forms, master Servant of Rudra changed the o of āṅ śasu into ava, and in his examples there is "sabaṃ saba," but citragau does not occur at all. These are the synonyms of Umā's name.

One verse gives the synonyms of Vināyaka's name. Because he is the chief of obstructors, he is Vināyaka: a compound of the pra class. Because he is lord of the obstructors, he is King of Obstructors (Vighnarāja). As before, ṭac and the like.

Two-Mothered: Īśvara had two wives, and one was the Gaṅgā. When a son was born to her, Umā went to see him and said, "Show me the son," but she said, "The son has no head," and did not want to show him. Umā grew angry and said, "Then let him be headless!" When they looked at the son, he had indeed become headless. When she then entreated Umā, Umā said, "Cut off the head of whatever corpse you find lying with its head toward the north and attach it, and the son will have a head." No human head was found there, but they came upon the corpse of an elephant; they cut off its head and attached it, and so he became elephant-headed, it is said. Thereafter, because he was raised by both Umā and Gaṅgā, he is Two-Mothered: by the rule "in the sense of descendant, from mātṛ after a numeral, sam, bhadra and the like," aṇ, and there is also ut. The hosts are the hosts of Pramathas, Caṇḍa and the rest. Because he became their lord, he is Lord of the Hosts (Gaṇapati).

Single-Tusked (Ekadanta): because the son of the Pleiades (Kārttikeya) pulled out his tusk, he is Single-Tusked. The story is this. When Umā's son was playing with Kārttikeya, Kārttikeya swung on his tusk, and it broke. Then, when he chased Kārttikeya, Kārttikeya fled to their mother, and the mother said, "The little one has no sense, so do not act like that." He said, "Then when he has grown big, I will kill him." She said, "So long as he has not reached the height of your knee, do not kill him." From then on Kārttikeya has always remained a youth.

Candrapīta, Mahānaḍau, Born of Śiva, Mountain-Binder, Jaṭāṭira, Atharvaranardha, Naguhakeśogra and Aged by Time are found in the Trikāṇḍaśeṣa.

He is Śaṅkara, and ramba is one who causes to dwell near: hence Heramba. By the rule "in the seventh, in many cases," the ending is not elided. From the usage of part and whole, he is also called Elephant-Faced (Gajānana). These are the names of the Lord of the Hosts (Gaṇapati).

Two verses give the names of the son of the Pleiades. The Pleiades (Kṛttikās) are the mothers who suckled him. For kṛttikā, in the sense of descendant, by the rule "dīpa ās ti ūṅ," ḍhak. The Host (Mahāsena): he possesses a great army. Because he was born in a reed thicket, he is Reed-Born (Śarajanman). Because he has six faces, he is the Six-Faced (Ṣaṇmukha). The Witherer (Skanda) withers the strength of the asuras; the suffix ac. Because he became the leader of the armies of the gods, he is the General (Senānī). He leads the armies, he pervades them: the suffix kvip.

Fire-Born (Agnibhū): long ago, when the Great God Rudra and Umā were in union, the fire god said, "If the seed goes into Umā's womb, an exceedingly mighty son will be born, so do not release it." Then, when Rudra spilled his seed upon the ground, Umā laid a curse: "Fire god, may it go into your womb!" So it went into his womb, and finding it utterly unbearable, he grew weary and distressed. He went to the goddess Gaṅgā, drew it out, and entrusted it to her womb. She went to a reed grove and gave birth there, so he is Born in the Reed Grove. Because it went into the fire god's womb, he is Fire-Born. And because the Pleiades suckled him and raised him, he is called the son of the Pleiades. From then on Īśvara too became known as Fire-Seed.

The Concealer (Guha) guarded the armies of the gods; by the rule "from a penultimate ik," ka. Born of Many (Bāhuleya): because he had six mothers in the Pleiades. In the sense of descendant, as before, ḍhak. Six-Mothered is also explained by this. Tāraka is a particular asura; because he slew him, he is the Tamer of Tāraka (Tārakajit). Because he was born at the time marked by the moon's conjunction with the asterism Viśākhā, he is Born under Viśākhā (Viśākha). Umā, the fire god, Gaṅgā, the reed thicket, the Pleiades, Viśākhā. By the rule "from sandhyā and the like, from an asterism," aṇ. By the rule "after batmaśā, from asterisms, in the plural," it is elided. By "luk of the elided aṇ and the like" and "goṇā of these," the dīpa too is elided.

The Crested One is the peacock, and he is the one who has it as his mount (Śikhivāhana). The Pleiades are six; therefore he is Six-Mothered (Ṣāṇmātura): in the sense of descendant, aṇ, as with Two-Mothered. By the rule "of jhal, before jaś, optionally ñam," it becomes ṇ. The short spear is a particular weapon; for the one who holds it, the suffix ac. The Youth (Kumāra): Brahmā granted him to be forever like a young boy. As the Skanda Purāṇa says:

He became the son of the god,
Endowed with great strength and power,
Ever beautiful like a child.
He is the son of Dharma,
Like a boy who has reached sixteen years;
For as long as time endures, sixteen years,
Like a boy who has reached sixteen years:
To show this, he is called the Youth.

In the Vāyu Purāṇa one hears that it is because his army of the gods took Śakra's daughter. It is also said that he remains a youth because, if he grew big, the Lord of the Hosts would kill him. Destroyer of Krauñca: he slew the asura called Krauñca, and the asura's wife says, "The Six-Faced One will kill him." For destroying him, by the rule "nandi and the like," lyu.

From "Indra" up to "Endowed with the Mountain-Born" are the names of Śakra (Indra). Indra, because he is king of the gods. Idi is in the sense of supreme lordship. From vasi, vaji, vṛdhi and indi, rak. Possessor of the Gods (Marutvān): he has the gods; marut too, since he has the gods, takes the suffix matus. By the rule "from jhal," it becomes v. Because he worships all the gods, he is the Worshipper (Maghavan): by the rule "śva and the like," from mahi, kanin, and there is also the change to maghaba. "Maghonī," with dīṣa in relation to a male, comes from Pūrṇacandra on the sūtra beginning "śva yuvan." How so? Maghavantau, maghavanto, maghavati, maghavad vajra lajjāni maghalbandanaṃ, or maghavadanaṃ: "magha is the sacrifice to the gods," says Pūrṇacandra in the Ally of the Roots. Because he has that, matus.

Biḍaujas, Pervasive Strength: he has strength that pervades everything; biṭ is pervasion and oja is strength, and since this too belongs to the pṛṣodara group, the letter is changed to o. Piva ti dru and dhuti; and from iṇ, bhī, kā, pā, śali and marci, kan. Pāka, "the Ripe," is the brother of the asura called Increase. Because he subdued him, he is the Destroyer of Pāka (Pākaśāsana): by "nandi and the like."

Listener to Elders (Vṛddhaśravas): Śakra has a retinue of a thousand sages; they are elders because they are learned. Since he listens and does whatever they command, he is Listener to Elders. Listener to Benedictions (Sunāsīra): the sages pronounce benedictions upon Śakra, such as "O god, may you be victorious! May you live long!" Nāśrī is in the sense of sound. Dantī and the like. By the rule "gambhīra and the like," īran. "Śakra, renowned like the sky, Listener to Benedictions" is composed in the Wondrous Sheaf. Only the rays of the body's giving are seen to go before the benediction: so master Prajñākaragupta, author of the Ornament of Kīrti's Intent, explained. Therefore the word aṃśu has the anusvāra in the middle, not the fifth letter of the ka group in the middle.

Because the asura called Puru, "the Full," called upon him, he is Called by Puru: so says master Śākyabuddhi in his extensive commentary on the Praise of the Wish-Fulfilling Jewel. For that very reason, since he slew him, he is also designated the Destroyer of Puru. Thus he is called the Destroyer of Puru and Called by Puru, as Servant of Rudra explained in his commentary on the Subanta of the Servant of the Delighter.

The Destroyer of Cities (Purandara): the cities of the asuras were destroyed by him. For "he destroyed cities," there is the anaṅ of "asun ṛt uśanas" and so on, and by "ac" and the like, after śa and ṣa, the second case ending too is elided. The Conqueror (Jiṣṇu) is one whose disposition is to conquer; for ji, ksnu. The Well-Inscribed are the gods, and he is their chief (Lekharṣabha). He is also called the Able (Śakra): by "śannoti," like rudra, the suffix rak.

Of a Hundred Sacrifices: according to the system of the tīrthikas, if one performs a hundred sacrifices one obtains lordship over the gods, and since Jiṣṇu Indra formerly performed a hundred sacrifices, he is known by this name. Here śatamanu means a hundred sacrifices. Śvek dibabti is Lord of Heaven. By the rule "in metrical usage, often," the sixth case ending is not elided. And metrical usage means that of the poets. Because it is a noun, "applied in explanation": this is what is meant here.

Because he protects the whole earth, he is the Good Protector (Sutrāman): for "he protects the ground well," kvip; by "vaci and the like," spati and manin. By the rule "in others too," the prior member is lengthened. Master Puruṣottama, in his commentary on the Uṇādi, gives sutrāma as an example, with the first vowel not long.

The Destroyer of Mountains (Gotrabhid): in former times, when the mountains went about, they crushed many people. Śakra struck them with his vajra and cut off their feet, and from then on they could no longer move. The mountain called Mīna fled and is in the ocean, so it is said.

The vajra is a weapon; and vasu means possessing wealth such as jewels. By the rule "jyotsnā and the like," aṇ in the sense of many. Because Śakra slew the asura called Increase, he is the Destroyer of Increase: for vṛtra and "destroys," kvip, as before.

The Rain-Sender (Vṛṣan): Śakra's cloud-elephant strikes the ocean and sends down rain from the sky. For "sends rain," from vṛṣi, dakṣi, rāji, dhanri, prati, dipi and yuba, kanin.

Vāstoḥ is the site, the ground; the lord is its presiding deity. Thus by his mantra one also makes it firm: he is the Lord of the Site, who makes the household settled and bright. Because, by the rule on particles, the sixth case ending is not elided, the s too becomes ṣ: vāstoṣṭhati. Further, Śakra is the lord of the sites of houses and the like; it is said that when one builds them, if one receives it from Śakra, he blesses and bestows it. He is the Lord of the Gods (Surapati).

Because the Lord of the Site slew the asuras called Bala, Increase, Jambha and Namuci, he is their enemy. She of Fine Speech (Śacī) is Śakra's wife, whose voice is exceedingly sweet; hence he is the Lord of Śacī. The Bay Horses are Śakra's own horses: it is said that Hari, Kanaka and Siṅgala are his horses. He is the King of Heaven (Svarāṭ): for "svaḥ rājati," kvip, and as before the r is elided and the vowel lengthened. On the view of the rule "after n, of the letter u, before ñam, optionally ñam," it is ṇ. By "na ṭo ra na bati," the n does not become yu.

Because he slew the asura Namuci, he is the Destroyer of Namuci. "From a penultimate ik": saki. Because of "nakha and the like," the n is not elided. For "he destroys him," by "nandi and the like," lyu.

"Thoroughly" (Saṅkrandana): because Śakra had slain all the sons of the asuras' mother called Diti, she was aggrieved, and she served the sage Kāśyapa with great devotion. He said, "What do you wish for?" She said, "I wish for one son able to overcome Śakra." He gave her a drop of water, and she conceived. Śakra heard of it and said, "I will destroy it right now." He entered her womb and cut the child into seven pieces, but it did not die, and it wept. Angered again, he cut each of those too into seven. Together they became the forty-nine wind gods. Then the mother said, "He makes it weep terribly." Therefore he is called the One Who Makes Weep.

Paralysed by the Hard-to-Bear (Duścyavana): there was a sage whose body was exceedingly hard to bear and who was old. The two sons of Aśvinī, called the Physicians of the Gods, healed him, and he became very young. Pleased, he said, "What do you wish for now?" They said, "We ask for a share of the fire offering," for by that, the outsiders hold, one obtains the rank of Śakra. When he was about to give it, Śakra grew angry and raised his vajra aloft to kill him, and Śakra's arm became paralysed. Therefore he is "Paralysed by the Hard-to-Bear," as is told in the tales of the Bhārata.

Enduring Others' Pride (Turāṣāṭ): Śakra is able to withstand the pride and arrogance of others, so says master Śākyabuddhi in his commentary on the Praise of the Wish-Fulfilling Jewel. By the rule "sahi: from tura saha la, ṇī," in metrical usage, ṇī. By the rule "in others too," the prior member is long. By the rule "often, in metrical usage," in the domain of jaś, the s that is not of the first ac becomes ṣ. "Turasāhau, turāsāhā": master Vāmana explains it thus in the sūtra that establishes ṣ here, "of sahi, from sāhā, s." There, its form becomes just as it is.

Rider on the Cloud (Meghavāhana): the elephant, son of Earth-Guardian, which bears a form like a great cloud, is his mount; by the mount, the lord is indicated. The All-Destroyer (Ākhaṇḍala): Śakra destroys all the forces of others; by the rule "from vṛṣa and the like, too," the suffix kala.

The Thousand-Eyed (Sahasrākṣa): the sage Gautama had a wife called Ahalyā, exceedingly lovely and charming. He hid her in the hollow of a tree, and she was unhappy. He said, "Go as far as here, but do not go beyond this." Then, after the sage had gone elsewhere, Śakra stayed with her. When the sage came back, Śakra made himself small and entered her womb. The sage asked, "Who came here?" and she said, "No one came."

The sage knew it and said, "I shall burn him with the fire of my curse," and Śakra, afraid, came out. Then the sage said, "Since you are the lord of the gods, I will not kill you, but may your body become a thousand vulvas!" Then his body became a thousand vulvas; and when he begged forgiveness, the sage turned them into a thousand eyes. From then on he is the Thousand-Eyed. Or else he is the Thousand-Eyed because he heeds the counsel of his retinue of a thousand sages, for the learned are like eyes. Akṣa means a sense faculty; as before, the suffix ṣac. He is also explained as Listener to Elders.

"Ṛbhukṣa is the vajra," says Pūrṇacandra in the Ally of the Roots. Others take the Mountain-Born to mean heaven. By the rule "often," the suffix ḍa. Because he possesses it, iṇi. For pathi, mathi and ṛbhukṣa, āt. By the rule "from śi and suṭ," of the letter i. These are the names of the lord of the gods.

His wife was born of the asura called Puloman, so she is Born of Puloman. "From bhū and the like, śaca is stated," explains Pūrṇacandra; and in the Illumination of the Roots, from that same root, śarci is cited as an example. For that same form, by "era kanti," dīṣa; this master Jayāditya cites as an example. Kauśika and Śacī, in the teaching of letters "kauśatakī," are also stated among the palatal ś. By "indra varuṇa" and so on, in relation to a male, dīṣa with an augment is correctly stated. Indrāṇī is also called Mahendrāṇī and Śakrāṇī in Rabhasa's lexicon. These are correct in the same way as Brahmāṇī.

His city is called Possessing the Immortals (Amarāvatī): for amara, in the sense of possessing, matus. The v of matu, and by "anajira and the like," the vowel is long. His horse is called Heard on High (Uccaiḥśravas): the suffix asun. Sūta means the charioteer. "Mātali is the one who drives the horses," says Rabhasa. Because it delights, it is the Delighter (Nandana): by "nandi and the like," lyu. Vaijayanta is that which is celebrated for its banners; here, by "arśas and the like," ac. For "it makes victorious," by the rule "from bhuji, vasi, vahi, sādhi, bhāsi, sahi, maṇḍi and hemi," the suffix anta. In the sense of a descendant of the Destroyer of Pāka, by "from at," iñ.

Earth-Guardian is the ocean, and because the elephant was born from it, by the rule "from the remainder," aṇ: he is the son of Earth-Guardian. The cloud-elephant is white by nature, like a mataṅga elephant. By "pṛṣodara and the like," airāvaṇa, the Lake-Born. The water-holder is the cloud, and he is also called its elephant. These are the synonyms of Earth-Guardian's son, irāpati.

One verse explains Indra's vajra. The Thunderer, because sound arises from the falling vajra. Ṇini is the suffix, and by its nature it is feminine. Because it goes on without anyone being able to destroy it, it is also called the Goer; like indra, rak. "Vajra, not feminine": "hīraka and pavi," so it is explained in the section on words of various meanings, and this connects with kuliśa. Kuliśa is explained as born of hrāda, and so construed. Kuli is the hand: "bhujādala," from the Trikāṇḍaśeṣa. It lies, that is, abides, in the hand: for śīṅ, the suffix ḍa. Or else kau means "on the earth," and niśyati "makes fine"; by "from a penultimate ik," ka. The Piercer has the nature of splitting; from vidi, bhidi and cchidi, kurac. The Thunderbolt is known as Indra's vajra: from puñ, like śuci, the suffix i. The Hundred-Pointed has a hundred edges or horns. Fierce Sound, because when the vajra falls the sound of thunder arises: svarati, śabhāyate. Often, from bhṛ, mṛ, tre, cari, tani, marji and śrī, u. Śam is in the sense of well-being; because it is not destroyed by anything, śamba. By "ti bhu ba yastā," ba. The Roar-Daunting terrifies with its sound: dambholi, of the pṛṣodara group. Because they occur together with śambha, dambholi and svaru too are masculine. From occurring together with svaru, koṭi; and from occurring together with śaṭakoṭi, pavi too. Likewise with others: by occurring together, their genders are understood from one another. For aśyate: this weapon devours the three worlds. For the Roar-Daunting and Fierce Sound: from ṛ, tṛ, pṛ, dhṛ, dhami, aśa, vipriti and grahi, ani. "Vajra" and "meteoric iron" are also its names. Vajra, meteoric iron, Mountain-Toppler, thunderbolt, Roar-Daunting, aśarbala and rakṣaja: these are Rabhasa's. "The vajra is meteoric iron; the vajra is the Piercer": from the Trikāṇḍaśeṣa.

"Sky-Vehicle, Sky-Dwelling" means "with a mansion": that which travels in the sky and abides there. "Not feminine" means not of feminine gender. "The divine sages, Nārada and the rest": the great sage Nārada, and, by the word "rest," Vyāsa, Tumburu, Kohala and others, the thousand who dwell in Indra's retinue. Sudharmā, the assembly hall of the gods, is the house where the gods gather.

The drink of the gods is amṛta: from pā, sautrāt, ri, makji, pīyi, hani and aṅgi, uṣan, siyaṣāṃ. In master Aruṇadatta's Instruction on Genders, on "jambāla, kuliśa, baliśa, anudarṣa, peyūṣaṃ," its commentator distinguishes: "here peyūṣaṃ is the nectar that is a transformation of fresh milk." Because it is the medicine against the māra of death, it is nectar. Sudhā, "Drunk with Ease": because by drinking this nectar the body is satisfied and one becomes deathless beyond measure, it is nectar.

The river of the gods is called the Slow-Flowing (Mandākinī), the Stream of Heaven, the Sky Gaṅgā, and the Long Essence-Bearer: these are the amṛtas that flow down from the summit of Sumeru. Because it has the nature of flowing slowly, ṇi is the suffix. By "svarṇadī la, girindī and the like," optionally ṇ.

It is also called Meru, Sumeru, the Golden Mountain, the Heap of Jewels, and the Abode of the Gods: these are names of Sumeru. Mīṅ is in the sense of harming. For pī and mī, ru. Su as the prefix. Other prefixes, pra and the like: because the sense is that the Mandākinī, the stream of heaven, has definitely entered it, pra-meru and the like are not formed. A mountain whose nature is gold is the Golden Mountain; its nature is precious. Here the word hema means jewel, by the figurative usage of cause for effect, just as, for example, a topknot of hair and the like is called hair and the like.

Next come the divisions of heaven and so on. Meru refers to mountains in general. Heaven is the top of Meru. The trees of the gods are those that have grown in the land of the gods. Mandāra is the tree that gladdens the mind; from agi, madi, mandi and kaṭi, aran. Pāriṇa is the ocean. "Born": in the sense of descendant and in its own sense, kan. The Well-Pervading is that which pervades completely, with its rising fragrance of incense; in the agent sense too, ghañ. The wish-fulfilling tree, because it arises from a true wish and is a tree by nature: the relation is characterised by the thing produced and the thing producing it. Because it captivates the senses and makes them delight in pleasure, it is haricandana; by "nandi and the like," lya. Or else "masculine" is connected with this. These are called the trees of the gods, from which all one needs and wishes for arises.

Haṃsago, druhiṇa and sanad are from Rabhasa as synonyms of Brahmā. His youth is his descendant. In the sense of descendant of the Arranger, aṇ. These sages are the physicians of heaven, and the authors of treatises too have set them down as the Physicians of the Gods.

She of the Horse, who took the form of a horse, is the name of the Sun's wife. Unable to bear the Sun's splendour, she took the form of a mare and dwelt in Uttarakuru practicing austerities. There, when the Sun was about to mount her in her horse form, she suspected him of being another male and turned her face back, and the seed went into her two nostrils. From it two sons were born, so they are called the Nose-Born. In Rabhasa's lexicon they are also called the Horse-Born and the Nose-Born. Because they fell from the nose: for sāṃta, without ktavantu, ṭa. By the rule "from verbal forms and proper names, often," shortening. For sata, su, bā, sādhū and sati, by the rule "good therein," yat. In the dual, a compound with nañ. Because of "nakha and the like," the n is not elided. Because they were born when the moon was in conjunction with the asterism Aśvinī, they are called the Aśvinī-Born. By the rule on the time of twilight, from an asterism, aṇ. By "batsa, śāla, from asterisms, in the plural," it is elided. By "the elision of aṇ and the like, luk, goṇya and the like," the dīpa too is elided.

They are also called the All-Dispensing, because they dispense everywhere to heal disease. From the root dasu, by "dur and the like," rak. By "dī ās ti ūṅ," ḍhak: āśvineyau, "the two sons of Aśvinī." The Nose-Born and the All-Dispensing are two brothers. As the Utpalinī says:

Yama and the Executioner,
Yama's sibling and the Nose-Born,
The All-Dispensing and Revanta:
These six by name are sons of the Sun.

And the Vāyu Purāṇa too says:

You are the Enemy of Increase and the Nose-Born,
And likewise the All-Dispensing and Varuṇa.

Here again the dual of hu too, as in "saptamī śauṇḍi śchantriṇo yānti" and the like, is stated as a dual expressing both by association with that one and by its word. For example, in the Bhārata: "These are two, the Utpahas and the Nose-Born, I hold." Likewise in the Māgha: "anusapra rusta matha, the two All-Dispensers, the sons." This is cited as an example of the single remainder where grace is not clearly in agreement. It is established by that as well. In the extensive commentary on the Abhidharmakośa, on "conditioned and unconditioned dharmas," master Yaśomitra explained, "in order to make known 'from guṇo yaṇuka.'" That a dual is used there serves to have each line of reasoning examined separately; I see no other purpose.

"Urvaśī and the rest" are the daughters of the gods, known as the courtesans of heaven. Because they are born from water, "water": from a particle, the uṇādi suffix asi. Or else, because they arise from water, they are called the Water-Arisen; the suffix asun. "Water-born" is plural because it rests on the individual, and the individual rests on its parts. Thus in Bhaṭṭa's system: "Words such as apsaras and dāra are applied sometimes to the class and sometimes to the individual. When they apply to the class, they take the number of the many individuals. When they apply to the individual, they take the plural number by the individual's parts, hands, feet and so on. When the word 'forest' applies to the class, it designates a qualified substance by a single number; or else, when it rests on individuals such as dhava trees, it designates the class: therefore it is singular." Vāmana and others hold "optionally plural"; thus "sand, rain, water flowers and water-moss are alike born of water," says Māna. For urvaśī, vāśa is in the sense of beauty. From uru, "great": mahato vaṣṭi, vaśīkaroti, ac. Also pṛṣodara and the like. Vasa is in the sense of dwelling. Because from the sage it becomes ur, urvaśi.

"Urvaśī and the rest" should be understood as follows: Lightning-Radiant, Tilottamā, Puṇḍarīkā, Fair-Armed, Chariot of Delight, Intoxicating, Menakā, Miśrakeśī, Well-Adorned, Muñja-Born, Alambuṣā, Plantain-Thighed, Sweet-Voiced and the like.

The singers of the gods are the gandharvas. Hā is an indistinct sound: by "jahāti kvipa vica," vic. Because sus and supa have the same meaning, it is a compound. By the rule "external," in an agent compound: for hvayati, in the sense of the particular sound of the song of the hanging rambha, kvip. By the rule "before kṛt too, of these," ik for yaṇ. By "from yaṇ ik," the following takes the form of the preceding. By "hal," lengthening. "These are like the words śubhaṃyā and khalapū," as the Servant of the Delighter explained, examining them in the Subanta. For one who sings, the singer: pṛṣodara and the like. Or else, because they feed on scents, they are scent-eaters; the suffix ac. "Gandhaḥ is a scent-eater, and gāndharva becomes the singer of the gods": so Rabhasa explains the form with the first vowel long as well. By the words "and the rest": Viśvāvasu, Cow-Life, the Delighter, Citraratha, Vessel-Bearer, Nārada, Haṃsa and others like them.

Having stated the lord of these together with his retinue, four verses beginning with "the Crooked-Goer" give the names of fire. Agni: it blazes and goes crookedly. From agi, ni. Fire was born from the sage Viśvānara; in the sense of his descendant, by "ṛṣi, kuru, vṛṣṇi, andhaka," aṇ. Wherever it arises it finds something to burn, so it is Find-and-Eat; from śṛ, śru, yu and vaha, ni. The Consumer: whatever the fire-offerer burns in it is consumed. From vī, "to go," and also in the sense of eating: "vī in the senses of going, coming forth, beauty, dwelling and eating," so it is explained; hence kanti. Because whatever substance is burned in it is consumed, it is Victor over Wealth. The Water-Womb, because at the time when the eon is formed it arose from within the great ocean. The Blazing is that whose nature is to blaze; by "jra, cuṃ, kramya, daṃ dvamya," uc. Obtained at Birth: from vindati, in the sense of obtaining, asun. Because it burns the body, it does not protect the body: by "jambala krisīṭi and the like," from tonoti, u; and by "it does not protect," napāt. From jvala: since it grows from wood, it is Wood-Increasing; by "jyoti and the like," isi. Because it dries up the waters, it is the Drier: for śuṣi, by "vyoma and the like," manin. Because the track of fire turns black, it is Black-Path. Because its light blazes like hair, it is Blazing-Haired; by "the latter, in a compound not bardhal," ṣ. Because fire offerings and the like are performed in the first watch, it is called Awake at Dawn; by "from a penultimate ik," ka; by "śuṣmā satpā diṣṭha har and the like," repha. "It eats its support": for the object, aṇ. Śuci, rkeśa: kṛśa is in the sense of making thin; it ends in a palatal. From hṛṣi, śuci, ṣkes and kṛṣi, kun. By "sum, anuka and the like," kṛ-anuka. Uṣ is in the sense of burning and budh in the sense of understanding; for pūnti, by "la," ṇvul. For ananti, jīvanti: bṛ uṣarbudhaḥ ṣa and the like; by "from some," kala.

Rohita means red: its horses are red. Thus the Śālihotra says:

Varuṇa's are white in color;
Śānu, Chung-gyé, and Kubera's are like the kumuda;
The fire god's have the light of the kiṃśuka, the ripener, pāvāka;
Likewise the wind god's are of varied color.

Because this is the friend of an, which sustains the wind, it is the Friend of the Wind; by "ṛ uśanas," anaṅ. The Crested is the blazing one. From śuṣi with āṅ before it, in the inner, desiderative sense, from the sanant form, by "uṇādi and the like, often," ina. Because it devours the grasses, it is the Grass-Eater, so says the Conduct of the Delighter.

Golden Seed: by Umā's curse, Rudra's seed lodged in its belly, and from it there came forth a youth of golden color, as is told in the Rāmāyaṇa episode. Therefore it is said, "The fire god's son was at first of golden color." The Eater of Oblations eats the sacrificial wood and the like. The Burner. The Conveyor of Offerings: what is burned in fire is received, it is said, by the gods and the ancestors. From havya, kavya, purīṣa and purīṣya, ñyuṭ. Or else, because it is to be received by the ancestors, vātya.

Seven Flames: it is said to have seven tongues of flame, which in the Mārkaṇḍeya Purāṇa are given as the Black One (Kālī), dahan havyavāhan the Terrifying (Karālī), Swift as Mind (Manojavā), Well-Beneficial, Fine Smoke-Colored (Sudhūmravarṇā), the Sparking (Sphuliṅginī), and Of Varied Light (Viśvaruci). Since these are by nature flames, it is Seven-Flamed. The Calmer burns thorns and the like; damu is in the sense of calming. For "it tames," by "un and the like, often," unsi. The Grief-Maker: when houses, wealth and the like are burned by it, people grieve; for śuca, by "bhadra and the like," rak. Among the divisions of its tongues, its rays are of varied light. Because of "kṣubhnā and the like," there is no ṭ. The Wealth of Light is that which possesses light. "It purifies": by "from a penultimate ik," ki.

Because it arose from drying up the waters of the great ocean, it is like the bile of the waters: Water-Bile. In a genitive compound, because the meaning of the latter member is principal, it is neuter: this very thing. Breast-Born: the sage called Ūrva, wishing for a son not born from a womb, churned upon his breast, and heat blazed up in it and a son was born. That fire abides in the ocean in the form of a mare. It is Breast-Born, and since fire was born from it, it is also called Mare-Born; this is appitti. For śuca. In the sense of a descendant of the sage Ūrva, by "ṛṣi, kuru, vṛṣṇi, andhaka and the like," aṇ: aurva. For vaḍava, by "from the remainder," aṇ. "The mare's fire" is a relation characterised by support and supported. "Worship of the gods": if one makes a sacrifice and offering into fire, the gods are worshipped, it is said.

"Both" means of both masculine and feminine gender. These are names of the blazing flame. The words arcis, jvāla and kīla mean the flicker, the blaze and the tongue of flame; they do not, like the word vahni, have one referent, but each denotes something separate. For jvalati, by "jvala and the like," optionally aṇ. Kīla is in the sense of binding; ac. By "from arcyate," from arci, hu, sṛpi and chardi, isi. The Lying One is the tongue of flame that appears as if lying down. "Feminine" is connected with arcis and the rest: since one is seen to be feminine, by force of their occurring together the others too are feminine. Further, spark and fire-speck are of all three genders. In the sense of the state of sphulati, by "from a penultimate ak," ki. By "sphuliṃ gacchati," khaṭ. By "sphulati," kvip. By "adgiti," ac. Or else it is a compound: "it is sphula and it is also adgi." By "kaṇati," ac.

The two quarter-verses are names of the heat born of fire: "it scorches all over," and "it strikes all over with fever"; in the sense of the action, ghañ. Shortening, for those that do not go. "Equal" means alike, that is, up to "in the same sense."

Two verses give the names of Yama. Because he makes Dharma paramount and metes out punishment according to the offense, he is the King of Dharma. As before, ṭac, as with tapa and jvara and the like. The departed fathers are the deceased forefathers. Their gods are Kavya, the Child, Nala, the Moon, the Slayer, the King of Dharma and the Bringer of Death; since he is the chief of these, he is their Lord. The Impartial, because without regard to friend or foe he judges even a king with punishment; since he has this disposition, the suffix ṇini, for vṛt. The departed are the dead, and he is their king; for rājate, kvis; by "praśca and the like," ṣ. The Ender (Antaka), because in the end he brings all people to death. He is known as the brother of the great river Yamunā. The Pacifier, because he brings all living beings to nothing: śamu is in the sense of calming; by "nandi and the like," lyu. The yamas, because they restrain creatures on every side, are the messengers of the Lord of Death and the like; he is their king, and the suffix is kvip. Because he possesses them, by "arśas and the like," ac: yama. For kalayati, "he reckons up the righteousness and unrighteousness of living beings," ghañ. The Staff-Holder holds the black staff that bears the tally of good and evil deeds; the suffix ac. The ancestors are the deceased forefathers and the like, and their god is Yama: when offerings are made to him, the departed obtain their share. Likewise he is also called the Son of the Sun: biśvanara is the Sun; in the sense of his descendant, before jita, aṇ. Because he brings living beings to their end, the suffix ḍa. These are the names of Yama of the southern direction.

A verse and a half give the names of Nairṛti's rākṣasas. Because one guards against them, they are rākṣasas: prajñā and the like. Because it has the disposition to eat corpses, by "chattra, viśvānara and the like," aṇ. From the usage of its disposition to eat corpses, the word kuṇapa is used. Kravya is flesh; for "it eats," by "kvipa vica" and the like, rākṣas, kvip. For the object, aṇ: kravyādaḥ. The commentator too, saying "optionally the form with as and aṇ is accepted," distinguished these by their meaning. In master Jayāditya's system, kvip is established by "not ending in at," and by saying "for kravya too," the purpose is to block the optional form with as. Kravya is uncooked flesh, or else repulsive transformations; by "pṛṣodara and the like," it is changed into kavya. Asra is blood. For "it drinks," from pā, "to drink," with no prefix and the like, ka. Because it roams everywhere, āśara. Because it does its deeds at night, it is the Night-Roamer and the Night-Goer: by "from rātri, optionally for the root," optionally num. Of Varied Color: its body is variegated; prabṛ is in the sense of color. By "often, makura, dardura, vidhura," u, urac and the augment r. That which kills and harms is the Killer: rātriñcara, rātricara, born of her very self. Because they seize and kill all who go about, they are the Seizers of Goers. Here the designation is expressed by the word for going: yātudhāna; from dhāñ, with ya at the start, lyuṭ.

Merit-Folk: the word "merit" is applied, by contrary indication, to those without merit. Where a word that by itself conveys its own meaning is applied to something else and indicates the opposite meaning, that is contrary indication. For example, calling the karaṇa Viṣṭi "the Good," and the planet Mars "the Auspicious," and like a woman who, having met with the worst of husbands, says, "Because I accumulated merit, I found a husband like you." Here the words "good" and "auspicious" mean what has merit, and yet the words "good" and "auspicious" also indicate what lacks merit. Because they dwell in Nairṛti's direction, by "from the remainder," aṇ. Or else, by "in the sense of descendant of Nairṛti," aṇ. The Follower follows all people looking for an opening: yā is in the sense of obtaining; by "yāti sarveṣāṃ" and the like, tu. The Guarded-Against: people, afraid of it, guard themselves with mantras, substances and the like. For "one guards oneself from it," asun. Thus the object is indicated by the subject. These are names of the rākṣasas, neuter because of the difference in form.

Half a verse gives the direction of the Lord of Waters. Because his mind is supremely good, he is the Supreme-Minded. Up to vṛṇvanti: because the gods granted him a boon, Varuṇa has the sense of "the choice," as the Sāmba Purāṇa says:

From the root, vṛ is explained as "the choice";
Therefore dāe is called "the choice."

Or else vṛṇoti, "he holds": from kṛ, vṛ, tṝ, dāri and arji, unan. Because he always holds a snake noose, he is the Bearer of the Snake Noose. He is the lord of all beings that dwell in the waters. Because he is the ground of the ocean, he is the Lord of Waters. These are the names of the guardian of the western direction.

That is Varuṇa. Now two verses are to state the guardian of the northwest, the wind. Because it makes beings breathe, it is the Breather; for śvasa, lyuṭ. Because it possesses the tangible qualities of cold, heat and the like, it is the Toucher; lyuṭ. For "it goes," from kṛ, vā, pa, ji, mi, svadi, sādhi and āśu, uṇ. After ā, before aṇ and al, yuk. Grown by the Mothers: it is said that it grows from the place where the seven mothers, Brahmāṇī and the rest, dwell. Sparśana is for spṛśa. From the root ṭuośvi, "to grow," by "śva and the like," kanin; with the particle, it is established as wind, and since it never stays still it is the Ever-Going: vāyu, mātariśvā. Babhrū is a color, also called pṛṣata. These are dappled horses, of a color having all three, white, red and yellow. As was said before:

The fire god's of the kiṃśuka's color,
Likewise the wind god's of varied color.

Because it is the vehicle of scent, it is the Scent-Bearer; from vahati, ac. As a name too it is vaha: "vaha, because it goes swiftly in the sky," in the Ratnakośa. Because it conveys scent, for the object, aṇ. By "sthira and the like," kic. Anila is the wind: kapirika and the like. Āśu means swift; gacchati, ḍa. Īr is in the sense of going; by "samīra," ac. From mṛ, uti.

Maruta. The god of the wind is māruta: prajñā and the like. "The Life of Beings" has a basis as a name; for anala and wind, one verse appears in an example:

Because it goes, it is the goer;
Because it dwells in the heart and moves swiftly, it is proclaimed "life."

So says Rudra. By "from gam, both too," kvip. Often, uk is treated as having the marker it. By "of gam and the like, before kvip," the m is elided. Jagan, jagantau. For praninti, "by this," ghañ. By "from an, also for anta," ṇ. Because it is the life of beings, it should be regarded as correctly construed as "the Life of Beings." For samīra, in the sense of a word for the disposition to move, from an intransitive verb, yuc. Then, because it is the lord that exists in the sky, matus. Or else, as the Vāyu Purāṇa says:

Sound is the power of the sky;
Likewise it makes puri arise.

"It has that": in the sense of matu. For vāti, from gāta, martavantana. Punāti, prabhapamīrṇanagni, and pavana, and prabhañjana, the Shatterer: nandi, somi and the like. From pūṅ of the bhū class, by the suffix śānac: the Full or the Purifier, pavamāna.

The winds dwelling in the body are of five distinct kinds, bearing names such as the life-breath, according to the divisions of their locations and functions. As it is said:

The life-breath is in the heart; in the anus,
The downward-clearing; purifier, shatterer; the equalizing in the middle of the navel;
The upward-moving is in the region of the throat;
The pervading, the full, the purifier, in all the joints of the body.

Of these, the life-breath brings about the taking in of food and the like. The downward-clearing expels feces, urine and semen downward. The equalizing brings about the digestion of food and the like. The upward-moving brings about speech, song and the like. The pervading brings about the flow of menses and blood, the opening and closing of the eyes, and the like. Since through it food and the like are taken in, it is the Drawer-In. Since through it feces, urine and the like are discarded, it clears downward. Since through it what is eaten is digested, it is the equalizing. Since through it the capacity for speech and the like is produced, it moves upward. Since through it menses, blood and the like are emitted, it pervades in particular. For all of them, prāṇa, apāna, vamāna, udāna and vyāna, the suffix ghañ.

The wind is that which goes swiftly and so on; and since it is always known by its very nature, its sequence is to be stated. Here, speed is force. All are formed in the sense of the means. The five from "the Mover" to "Force-Going" are names of rushing. "Then" has the sense of atha. Swift, fast, without tarrying, quick, in a trice, at once, unhindered, immediately, in an instant, without lingering, and briefly: these indicate merely swiftness in general. Hari is in the sense of going; asun. Raṃha: for taranti, "by this," asun. Raya is in the sense of going; gha. Or else ri is in the sense of hearing. By "syada in the sense of running," ghañ; from the particle, syada of syanda. Ju, a root of the sūtras, in the sense of speed, is read here as "ju cam kramya"; by ṛt and ut, as. Those ending with āśu are names of swiftness: the distinction between "swift" and "fast." "Fast" and the like express going with force; "swift" and the like express a property. Thus one uses "śīghraṃ pacati" and "śīghraṃ gacchati," but not "javaṃ gacchati" or "javaṃ pacati."

By "bhadra and the like," from śīṅ, rak, and also the augment ghuk. Or else from śī, kvip; and for "lying in bed," by "jighati," ka. Tvara is in the sense of hurrying. By "in the sense of going, and intransitive," often, kta in common with time. By "from ruṣa, hṛṣa, amara, tvara, saṃghuṣa, sra na," śīghraṃ, or else aṭ. By "śaku and the like," from raghi, ku. Laghuḥ: kapirika and the like. Like rudra, for kṣipa, rak. Ṛ and sṛ are in the sense of going; by "iyanti," ac. Svarita. Because it proceeds together with haste, satvaraṃ: saha is changed to sa, a compound referring to another. Cupa is in the sense of going slowly; because of "vṛṣa and the like," kala. Often, from kṣapripo, the u also becomes a. Unhindered in eating. By "jvara tvara," with ūṭh. Ra dātu ta. "At once": by "boto daṣca," n. Tūrṇaṃ. For "without lingering" and "unhindered," labi is in the sense of going; kta. By "kṛ, vā, pā, ji," from aśnāti, uṇ: āśu.

From "continually" up to "not occasionally" are names of the perpetual and continuous. Santate means "continually": from saṃtanoti, kta. By "optionally in the sense of going," avilambita, the state of ḍa. By "for all," elision is optional. For "continuous" only: anāratā, virata, navaratā; with the agent ending, a compound with nañ. Ra preceded by āṅ and the like has the sense of resting; thus in the miscellaneous section it explains ārati, avarati, sirati, uparati, uparame. Because there is nothing bad in it, it is the Faultless.

Excellence: atiśayate, erac. Bhṛ is in the sense of brgyad bkag; kṛ and the like. In the sense of the action, by ṛt and ut, ap. In the senses of time, custom, overpowering in sequence and great intensity, a compound with pra and the like. Thus "exceedingly" has the sense of "measure," and "measure" means a little; or else it is in the domain of determining the meaning. Bhṛśu and bhraṃśu are in the sense of falling down; for bhṛśyati, "makes low," by "from a penultimate ik," ka. From gāhu, kta: udgāḍhaṃ. By "bhadra and the like," for tīva, iran. Ekonto tra. Tamu is in the sense of desiring; kta. By "from ñam, before kit," lengthening. By "of m too, n," the m becomes n. The living is complete. The opposite of that: applied to a mere property, śīghra and the rest, up to "firm," are neuter. "He cooks excessively": as before, with the ending ac in the agent sense, it expresses a qualification of the action. "Eats quickly," "always performs the fire offering," "always rejoices": because these qualify the action, they are established as neuter. Thus the words from "Excellence" up to "firmly too" have the sense of "exceedingly": the sense of doing something more and more, again and again.

"Swift" and the like, when applied to what has no mind, are of neuter gender. "Swift" and the like, when applied to what has a mind, take all three genders. "Fast" and all the rest are explained here, grouped under the wind.

Two verses explain Kubera, the guardian of the northern direction. Kubera has three legs and eight fangs, exceedingly terrifying and ugly. Ku is a word indicating badness, and bera means body: his name is "Bad Body," so says the Vāyu Purāṇa. The Three-Eyed is Maheśvara. Just as he practiced austerities and vows, Kubera too practiced austerities and vows, and so the Three-Eyed came and said, "Since your austerity equals mine, we two are friends." From then on he became known as the Friend of the Three-Eyed. As before, ṭac and the like. He is the king of all the yakṣas: for rājate, kvip. The guhyakas are those who hide treasures, and he is their lord. Having Human Nature: his activities and conduct accord with those of humans. Because he carefully guards wealth and bestows it, he is the Giver of Wealth: for dā, without pra and the like, ka. Because he is the lord of the yakṣas, he is the King of Kings: "rāja means both yakṣa and king," from the Trikāṇḍaśeṣa. As before, ṭac. He is also called the Lord of Treasures and the Lord of the Kinnaras.

Because he is the son of the sage called Viśravas, he is Vaiśravaṇa: from the word viśravaṇa, by "śiva and the like," aṇ. His grandfather is the sage Vast-Light; hence he is the Grandson of Vast-Light. For bula, maha la, kvip. Asa, "blazing," and both are distinguishing words of bhū and the like. Thus: "as in the senses of going, blazing and giving." For the feminine, kanti. Because his light is vast and inwardly clear, he is Vast-Light; in the sense of his descendant, by "garga and the like," yañ. Riding on a Man: Īśvara gave him a man who bore the liṅga, and from then on he became known as Riding on a Man. Yakṣa is in the sense of worship. Of the remainder of "cura and the like." For the object, ghañ.

Tawny-Eyed: Vaiśravaṇa saw Gaurī, who was exceedingly beautiful, and, filled with desire, gazed at her; her radiance dimmed and ruined his eye. Rudra said, "Do not do that!" and made his eye reddish-tawny: so goes the ancient tale. Descendant of Ilavilā: he is the grandson of the one called Ilavilā, wife of the sage Vast-Light and daughter of the royal sage Tṛṇabindu. In the sense of her descendant, by "of nadī, mānuṣī and the like," aṇ. The Giver of Fortune bestows enjoyments; it has the suffix ka. Merit-Folk are the rākṣasas, and he is their lord. These are the names of Vaiśravaṇa.

"His" is connected all the way up to "Puṣpaka." "Citraratha is his grove": this is his pleasure garden, made by the gandharva king called Citraratha; the suffix aṇ. His son is called Nalakūbara: marked by Śrī, or adorned with excellence. Nala is a division of lord of men: naḍa. Kūṅ is in the sense of sound; by "sampad and the like," kvip. For "by it one holds, one rejoices," by "graha, vṛ, tṛ," as. Caitraratha, nalakūbaraḥ.

His Abode of Play and his City,
And his Sky-Dwelling is Puṣpaka.

So it is stated. Here, because it is used for play, "play": by "cūḍa and the like," aṇ. Because one dwells here, it is an abode; it is an abode and it is also play, so it is the Abode of Play. Like "black snake," it is always a descriptive compound. Ala is in the sense of ornament; kvun: alakā. Because of "kṣipakā, alaka and the like," there is no i. Puṣpa is in the sense of blooming; kvun.

Kinnara, Horse-Faced and mayu are given as synonyms in the Ratnakośa, Bikhāla. The kinnara, "is it a man?", is somewhat like a human. The kiṃpuruṣa, "is it a person?", is not human either. Horse-Faced: some, the males, have horses' faces, while the females have human faces and bodies shaped like horses; some are the reverse. By the usage of the whole that possesses parts and of the collection, these are "Horse-Faced." They are Kubera's retinue.

Treasure means a great inexhaustible heap of wealth. Naya is in the sense of going; by "śapta and the like," ku. For pra and the like, from dā and dhā, ki. Śebṛ is a root in the sense of continuing; for it, by "tālavyādi and the like," a. For śevadhīyate, by "also for object and locus," ki. By "from the proper name of dyāyo, ... nidhi in the sense of treasure," shortening. By the maxim of the crow's eye, "masculine" connects with both nidhi and śevadhi. Its divisions are nine; śibadhi means treasure or storehouse: Padma, Mahāpadma, the Conch, the Makara, the Tortoise, the Drum, the Joyful, the Blue, and the Producer. These are the nine treasures. "His" means the treasures are Kubera's storehouse. With these, the guardians of the directions ending with Vaiśravaṇa have been explained, together with their accessories.

Now come the synonyms of the upper direction, the sky. Because it is the place where the gods play, it is the Gods' Playground; it is also called the Place of Sport. "Both, feminine" means that they are of feminine gender. Because the sky does not move anywhere, it is the Unmoving: by "it does not go," ḍa. Abhra: the Unmoving. Or else, because it possesses non-going, by "arśas and the like," ac. Because it pervades all entities and non-entities, it is the All-Pervading: by "vyoma and the like," from vyañ, manin, and also o. When flowers and the like arise, the earlier bhramu, "wandering," is removed and a is put in its place. Because it gives room to grow, it is the Nourisher, puṣkara: often, by "śiraḥkarṇa," karna; from puṣ. The Resounder: the sound is the cloud of the sky. For "here it resounds": often, from bhrami, dhabi, debi and vāsi, aran. Asun. Nabhaḥ means the sky. Because in between, here, one goes and looks, it is the Intermediate Space. Sky and intermediate space. Further, the All-Going gives room for going. For gacchati: from all, often; raśi, ruci; from vṛñ, uc; am is elided, and there is also the augment agaṭ. Because the sky has no end, it is ananta. Because it is the path of the gods, it is the Gods' Path. For khanati, gagana: by "kriyā dhānaṃ," ḍa. For viyacchati: from "no paramate yami," kvip.

By the rule "gam and the rest, before kvip," the m is elided. The augment tuk. Heaven, the Unmoving, and the Thoroughly Pervading Viṣṇu, that is, Viṣṇu's Footstep.

Kāśṛ is in the sense of shining. For "here things are made clearly manifest," in the sense of the word kha, ghañ. According to Candragomin, ākāśa, kuśa and aṅkuśa are written with the palatal ś.

For vihāyas: "specially" is vi, and hayate is in the sense of going everywhere; haya is in the sense of going. Asun. By "also in the others," the vowel is lengthened. Because it is unobstructed, it is the Released. "Or in the masculine" means that the All-Illuminated, ākāśa, may also take the masculine gender. These are the names of the sky.

With half a verse come the names of direction in general. Because it points out those things, it is direction. By the statement "from dik and the rest," kvin. For kakubh: by "kena vātena kṣubhyate," "by which wind is it stirred," kvip. Or else, because by the sun anything whatever is made clear and visible: from the root kṣumbhi in the sense of being clear, kvip. Because it belongs to the class pṛṣodara and the rest, a letter is elided.

Because, when the sun and the other luminaries and constellations rise, the directions east, west and so on come into being, it is the Showing of Two. Kāśṛ is in the sense of shining. From rami, kubi and kāśi, kthan. By the rule beginning with ṣṭunā, there is the ṣ and the ṭh, giving kāṣṭhā. "Kena sūryeṇa kṣubhā," "by which sun is it stirred," is the same. Because it shows by way of direction, it is the Place of Clarity.

Aśū is in the sense of pervading. With ā, in the sense of "all around," as the qualifier, ac: āśā, the All-Pervading. For tarati: by "from jvala, namāṃsi, hṛ, sṛ, tahi, ruhi and muhi," harit. Because whatever direction the mind is set on, it clings to that one, it is the Ravisher. These words, direction and the rest, are feminine.

Morning and the rest are particular directions. Because the sun rises in the morning, it is the east. By midday, the south. By the afternoon, the west. Vyanakti: hṛ, with ta and the augment iṭ. That is direction. Because the sun, after setting, is seen as though travelling on through the sky, the north is called the upper direction. "The directions become of the third" means that, as adjectives, they take all three genders, masculine, feminine and neuter.

The directions east and the rest are to be known through the direction guardians, Indra and the rest, because they are their lords. The eight elephants that stand guard in the four directions and the four intermediate directions are these. In Indra's quarter is the one called Ocean-Born, Airāvata, son of the Earth-Guardian, that is, of the ocean. In Agni's quarter is the one called Possessor of the White Lotus, Puṇḍarīka. In Yama's quarter is the one called Left-Sided, Vāmana, a name meaning very beautiful. In Nairṛti's quarter is Earth-Delight, Kumuda. In the quarter of the Lord of Waters is the Anointer, Añjana. In the quarter of the wind is Flower-Tusk, Puṣpadanta. In Kubera's quarter is the one Renowned Through All the Earth, Sārvabhauma. In Īśāna's quarter is the one called Possessor of Beautiful Limbs, Supratīka. These are the eight elephants of the directions. Their wives are eight she-elephants: in the east the she-elephant Cloud-Holder, Abhramu, and the rest dwell in due order.

"Non-direction" is a name of the intermediate quarter. "Neuter, indeclinable" means that it is of neuter gender and indeclinable. Because it lies in the middle of each pair of directions, it is the Midst of Directions. Because it lies at their junction, it is the Intermediate Direction. These are the names of the intermediate direction.

With two quarter-verses come the names of the encircling horizon. The encircling rim and the surrounding edge are the adornment of the Cakravāla, the iron ring of mountains.

From "Water-Holder" up to "garland of clouds" are names of the cloud. The Water-Holder is a massing of fine particles of water: "from ap, in the sense of holding, bhra." So says the master Puruṣottama in the commentary called Bhāṣāvṛtti, under mūlavibhuja and the rest.

Because rain comes from it, it is the Water-Pourer: for mehati, ac in the sense of causing change, and by nyaṅku and the rest, megha. Because water comes from it, it is the Water-Pourer, with aṇ for the object. Because thunder comes from it, it is the Resounder: from the word stana, for "it makes that resound," from hṛṣi, yuti, giti and madi, itnuc. The Water-Pourer is itself the Water's Mount, by pṛṣodara and the rest. Because it holds streams of water, it is the Stream-Holder, ac. Possessor of Lightning: matup in the sense of possession, and by the rule ending "jhayaḥ," the v. Because it gives water, it is the Water-Giver: for dadāti, ka. Because it bears water, it is the Water-Bearer: from bhṛ, kvip. Vāhaka. For "it destroys torment": by "mūrtau ghanaḥ," from han, ap, and also the change to the guttural.

Because by it the root of water is bound, or else because it brings water forth and lets it drip, with the suffix ac, it is jīmūta, by pṛṣodara and the rest. Because, when it gathers, it gladdens all creatures, it is the Gladdener: by "sthira and the rest," kirac. "Scatterer of the waters": jīva is water; mūtrayati and śravati mean causing to drip; these combined become jīmūta. Hence the Water-Releaser: for jalaṃ muñcati, "it releases water," kvip.

Smoke-Womb: because smoke is its lineage. When smoke gathers it turns into cloud, just as with quicksilver. In the Mud-dga'-ba the causes of a cloud are water, wind and smoke. Heron-Abode: because the bird, the heron, always dwells together with the clouds. The cloud garland is a mass of clouds. These are names of the cloud.

From "Cloud-Born" up to "Fierce Sound itself" are names of thunder. What arises from cloud is the Cloud-Born. "And the rest" means the Resounder, the Sound-Emitter, and so on.

From "Happiness-Drinker" up to "the Restless" are names of lightning. The Happiness-Drinker: because when lightning appears it terrifies people and dries them up, the suffix ki. Because its sound is a hundredfold, it is the Hundred-Sounding: because it belongs to pṛṣodara and the rest, the vowel is short. Śatahradā. It is also called the Sound-Scatterer: for saṃhrādate, from grahādi and the rest, ṇini.

The king of the clouds, the great cloud that dwells above all others, is called the Water-Born, Airāvata. What dwells together with it in one place is airāvatī: by "in the remaining senses," aṇ. Because its light lasts but an instant, it is the Instant. Because it strikes, it is lightning, like the word harit, the Ravisher; so it is said. "White Twice-Drinker" too is given in the Trikāṇḍaśeṣa as a synonym of airāvatī. In one part of it is saudāmanī, the Brilliant, with aṇ as before. In aṇ, the a is elided, and the elision of the final vowel and what follows it, the ṭi, does not take place. "Shining is said of the lotus; moving is said of the sky": so it is also applied in the Rabhasa. Because it shines and flickers, it is lightning, with kvip. It is also the Mover and the Restless: from cañci and capi, in the frequentative sense, as a noun, alac.

"Sound of the thunderbolt" and "sound of the vajra" are names of thunder that comes with a thunderbolt. "Cloud-Ray" and "Water-Holder-Born" are names of the thunderbolt, the sky-iron, which arises from clouds clashing with one another. Some thunderbolts flicker and are obstructed by nothing at all; though they pierce a house, they leave no trace. Others again destroy mountains, houses and the like. Though it is fire, it is not quenched by the waters of the ocean and the other waters, yet it is quenched by liquor.

"Indra's Weapon" and "Śakra's Bow" are names of the rainbow. Śakra is Indra, lord of the gods. "Straight" and "crooked": it is straight when it has no bends, and crooked when it curves to one side.

"Rain" and "shower" are general names of rain. In the feminine, ktin: vṛṣṭi, in the sense of raining. In the sense of the bare action, ghañ: varṣa. Its obstruction, moreover, is when some god or other being stops the rain from falling. "Alike" means that the two words have the same gender.

"Stream-fall" and the other two are names of a heavy downpour. What comes forth and falls with the full force of streams of water is the downpour: sṛ is in the sense of going, with ghañ.

"Rain-dust" and the rest are names of fine spray. From śīkṛ, like ambara, aran: śīkara.

"Vajra-rain" and "hail", the two, are names of hail. Kṛñ is in the sense of harming. From kṛñ and the rest, vun. Because it belongs to kṣipaka and the rest, the i does not replace the a. Vajra-rain is hail. Kara is explained by the Rabhasa as meaning hail, ray and hand.

"Cloud-massed" and "Maker of a Bad Day" are names of overcast weather. Because it is bad and despised, it is a bad day: a compound of the class of pra and the rest.

From "darkness" up to "covered" are general names for what is concealed from view.

With two verses come the names of the moon. Hima means cool; it is the moon's rays. Cadi is in the sense of gladdening. Because it belongs to dūra and the rest, rak in the sense of the action: candra, the moon. Because it appears like camphor in color and qualities: from candra and mā, by the rule given as "ang it," asi. Cadi is in the sense of gladdening and also of shining. Because roots have many meanings, for "it shines, it gladdens," by the list beginning sphāyi, rak. From indi, like vasu, the suffix u: indu. It is the Friend of the Water-Lilies, because it awakens them and makes them bloom. Because it is marked by nectar and enjoys it, it is the Nectar-Rayed. The Lord of Plant Juices is lord of all that grows as fruit and the like. The Ocean-Born is born from the churning of the ocean.

Because it sustains the gods, it is the Sustainer: for jīva, by "yuka and the rest," kan; and by "the augment ātṛk," also the ai. Ṣuñ is in the sense of pressing out: sunoti. From ṛ, stu, su, dhṛ, kṣi, kṣubh, pā and the others, man: soma. Soma is the Arouser of Passion, for it is said that when the moon rises, passion grows. Because it wanes entirely, it is the Waner: from glā and nud, ḍau: glau. Because a beast is its mark, it is the Deer-Marked; this is known from the birth-story of the bodhisattva as a hare. Because it is the treasury of the digits, it is the Treasury of Digits.

The twice-born is a brahmin. He has two births: he is born of brahmin parents, and he is born of the observance when he takes up the brahmin's vow. If he does not take up the observance, he does not attain brahminhood, even though his parents are brahmins. Hence the moon is called King of the Twice-Born, as it is said:

The lunar mansions, the planets, and the brahmins,
the medicinal herbs, the sacrifices and the nights:
as king over all of these
the brahmins appointed the moon.

Śaśadhara is the Hare-Bearer, because it bears the form of the bodhisattva who became a hare. Nakṣatreśa is Lord of the Lunar Mansions, because it holds sway over the lunar mansions, Rohiṇī and the rest. Kṣapākara is the Night-Maker, because it gives the night its character.

For the moon's body there are two words: bimba, image, and maṇḍala, disc. Kalā is a digit, and "ṣoḍaśo bhāgaḥ" means the sixteenth part, because the moon is made full by its sixteen digits. That same part is also called piece, half and portion. These are names connected with the moon's body.

Moonlight has three names. Candrikā is the Moon-Possessing, because, surrounding the moon, it makes things appear distinctly. Kaumudī is called Benefactor of the Water-Lily, because it benefits the kumuda flower. Jyotsnā is the Luminous, because it has light. The clarity of moonlight has two names: prasāda, clarity, and prasannatā, limpidity.

With two half-verses comes the mark on the moon. Kalaṅka is the Spotted, also explained as "making dusty" and "making turbid."
