Mtshan in "names of the Buddha" means names, not the major marks.  In the opening verse, "marks of the Guide" renders the Sanskrit word written bi nā ya, taken as vināyaka, the epithet "Guide" explained in the preceding piece; the reading is uncertain. "The Tantrapradīpa" takes the title as Maitreyarakṣita's grammatical work, Rakṣita being the master named; the title as written could also be read "Clarification of the Tantra." The affix written bod, the king's name Kecana, and the forms "Knower of the Stream" and "Abandoner of Evil" (apparently Tibetan renderings of names in Pāṇini's list of Atri, Bhṛgu and others, possibly Kutsa and Vasiṣṭha) could not be securely identified and are given as written or translated literally. "Sarvārthasiddha" follows the lexicon's entry and the derivation given; the Tibetan reads simply "Accomplisher of Aims." In the cosmogonic verse, "Sacrifice" renders the word elsewhere used for the sage Kratu; here it refers to Brahmā born from the egg. The garbled syllables after "knows all phenomena" are read as a causal ending. "The one who gives a bride" renders a phrase that may correspond to the "initiator" of the Sanskrit verse on the five fathers.  This piece is dense grammatical commentary on the Amarakośa's names of Brahmā and Viṣṇu; Sanskrit grammatical forms are given as the Tibetan spells them, and several of the cited rules are garbled in transmission and are rendered as written. The masters "Sambalī" and "Rabhasa" and the forms "joining five" and "kinta" in Ratnamati's explanation are uncertain. "Ātmabhū" is kept for consistency with Brahmā's epithet; in Viṣṇu's list the lexicon reads svabhū, of the same meaning. The asura "Giver" is a literal rendering of the Tibetan; it evidently stands behind the derivation of Daitya. The four-line verse on Upendra is obscure and is translated as closely as the Tibetan allows.  This piece glosses the Amarakośa's names of Viṣṇu, his father Vasudeva, his brother Balarāma, and Kāma; the Tibetan headwords translate the Sanskrit names, which are given here. Several cited grammatical rules are garbled in transmission and are given as the Tibetan spells them. The Tibetan for Kaṃsārāti reads literally "the sound of bronze," and the headword before Adhokṣaja in the Ratnakośa list is corrupt; both are read from the lexicon's entries. The Tibetan for Kandarpa literally means "Crusher of Brahmā," reflecting the derivation the commentary gives. The nāga Kālindī follows the Tibetan; in Sanskrit sources Kālindī is the Yamunā river itself.  This piece glosses the Amarakośa's names of Kāma, of his son Aniruddha, of Lakṣmī, of Viṣṇu's emblems and of Garuḍa. Many cited rules and Sanskrit forms are garbled in transmission and are given as the Tibetan spells them; the run quoted from Rakṣita on the vocative of lakṣmī is unintelligible as written. The fourth arrow's name has extraneous syllables inserted in the Tibetan; "the Drier" renders its evident sense. "Lord of Supreme Delight," "the Servant of Rudra" (author of the Answers to Questions), and "the Companion to the Roots" are translated literally because the names could not be securely identified. "His son Aniruddha" reads the evident bu ma 'gags pa for the written bum 'gags pa. The sentence calling the sword "Pāñcajanya" follows the Tibetan, which seems to have carried the conch's name over.  This piece glosses the Amarakośa's names of Garuḍa and of Śiva (Īśvara). "Kadrū" identifies the Tibetan "Auspicious One," Vinatā's rival in the wager over the color of the sun's horse, as the nāga-mother of the familiar story; the Tibetan name itself means "Auspicious." Garuḍa's two elder siblings are given as the Tibetan has them (lightning and the dawn star). In the Viṣṇuratha story the Tibetan says only that Viṣṇu "put something in his nose and made him unchanging," evidently a nose-rope. The Tibetan for Śarva is the same word used earlier for Kāma's epithet Māra; here context requires Śarva. Several syllables in the Śrīkaṇṭha story are corrupt ("Īśvara" and "beryl" have stray syllables inserted) and are read in their evident sense; some cited grammatical rules are garbled and are given as written.  This is a lexicographical commentary on the Amarakośa list of Śiva's names (virūpākṣa, trilocana, kṛśānuretas, sarvajña, dhūrjaṭi, nīlalohita, hara, smarahara, bharga, tryambaka, tripurāntaka, gaṅgādhara, andhakaripu, kratudhvaṃsin, vṛṣadhvaja, vyomakeśa, bhava, bhīma, sthāṇu, rudra, umāpati; kaparda, pināka/ajagava, pramathas, mātṛkās). Many Sanskrit grammatical citations are garbled in the Tibetan transmission; they are given as written. Likely intended forms: vitupākṣa for virūpākṣa; asa for ap (Pāṇini's ṛdor ap) and asi/asihita for api/apihita (ས for པ); kasarda for kaparda; sāriṣa/sāriṣada for pāriṣa/pāriṣada; tudra for rudra; dīpa for ṅīp; śakantu for śakandhu. The source titled Sāsbrasuraṇa is probably a Purāṇa (perhaps a slip for purāṇa) and is not identified. "Of ancient lineage, the Trunk" stands out of sequence and seems to be a stray gloss on Sthāṇu; likewise "Bhava" before "the Abider" appears to be a stray repetition. "The horse" in the verse on Nīlalohita is as written (རྟ), though fire would be expected. In the Rudra passage, དུས and དུ་བྱེད are read as ངུས / ངུ་བྱེད, "wept" / "the Weeper," as the etymology from rud requires. The doubled "it is known" after the Umā story is a dittography, translated once. "The sage called the Clear One" (གསལ་བ) is in context Dakṣa, but the Tibetan name is translated literally since the rendering is unusual. The asura Tutu is unidentified (the myth of the mothers usually involves Andhaka). འཇོམས་བྱེད here glosses bharga (the Destroyer), not the Pramathas, who are here རབ་འཇོམས; and མཚོན་བྱེད here is the technical "illustration" (lakṣaṇa), not Lakṣmī; མདའ here is "arrow," not the name Bāṇa.  The passage continues the commentary on the Amarakośa: the mothers, the eight powers of lordship (aṇimā and the rest, following the Sāṃkhya commentator Jayamaṅgala on the Sāṃkhyakārikā, "the Seventy"), the names of Umā (Umā, Kātyāyanī, Gaurī, Kālī, Haimavatī, Īśvarī, Śivā, Bhavānī, Rudrāṇī, Śarvāṇī, Sarvamaṅgalā, Aparṇā, Pārvatī, Durgā, Mṛḍānī, Caṇḍikā, Ambikā) and the names of Gaṇeśa. Many Sanskrit grammatical citations are garbled in transmission and are given as written (for example dīṣa for ṅīṣ, ṭāraci, yābabī, sabaṃ saba). Here དབང་ཕྱུག in "qualities of lordship" renders aiśvarya, not the name Īśvara. In "praised by one's retinue," བརྗེད ("forgotten") is read as བརྗོད ("praised"). In the verse, ཁྱབ་འཇུག stands for the goddess Vaiṣṇavī, and "Withering One" and "Moon Goddess" translate སྐེམ་བྱེད་མ and ཟླ་བ་མ, which are not identified with certainty. Among Umā's names, དྲག་མོ is Rudrāṇī (the wife of Rudra), whereas in the list of mothers it was rendered Raudrī. "She goes as the Fair-Faced One" (བཞིན་བཟང་མར་འགྲོ) is unclear. སློབ་དཔོན་སྲུང་བ is taken as the grammarian Rakṣita (perhaps Maitreyarakṣita); "Servant of Rudra" and "the Sheaf of Forms" (perhaps a Rūpamañjarī) are translated literally because they cannot be identified with confidence. "Caṇḍa" among the Pramathas reads གཏུམ་མ, apparently for གཏུམ་པོ as in the previous piece.  The passage comments on the Amarakośa names of Gaṇeśa (Ekadanta, Heramba, Gajānana), of Kārttikeya (Kārttikeya, Mahāsena, Śarajanman, Ṣaḍānana, Skanda, Senānī, Agnibhū, Guha, Bāhuleya, Tārakajit, Viśākha, Śikhivāhana, Ṣāṇmātura, Śaktidhara, Kumāra, Krauñcadāraṇa) and the first names of Indra. In the tusk story the Tibetan uses སྨིན་དྲུག ("Pleiades") as shorthand for Kārttikeya; the sense, confirmed later in the passage, is that Gaṇeśa threatened to kill Kārttikeya if he grew up, so he remains a youth. Many grammatical citations and Sanskrit forms are garbled in transmission and are given as written. The names attributed to the Trikāṇḍaśeṣa at the start are given as spelled, two of them translated. In "since he has the gods," ལྷོ is read as ལྷ. "Endowed with the Mountain-Born" (རི་སྐྱེས་ལྡན), the last of Indra's names, probably renders Ṛbhukṣan. "The asura called Increase" (འཕེལ་བ), "the Wondrous Sheaf" and "the Ally of the Roots" (perhaps Pūrṇacandra's Dhātupārāyaṇa) are translated literally since they cannot be identified with confidence. The Vāyu Purāṇa remark about "his army of the gods" taking Śakra's daughter probably alludes to Devasenā, Indra's daughter and Skanda's consort. Prajñākaragupta's "Ornament of Kīrti's Intent" is his Pramāṇavārttikālaṅkāra.  The passage continues the Amarakośa names of Indra (Puruhūta, Purandara, Jiṣṇu, Lekharṣabha, Śakra, Śatamanyu, Divaspati, Sutrāman, Gotrabhid, Vajrin, Vāsava, Vṛtrahan, Vṛṣan, Vāstoṣpati, Surapati, Balārāti, Śacīpati, Jambhabhedin, Harihaya, Svarāṭ, Namucisūdana, Saṅkrandana, Duścyavana, Turāṣāṭ, Meghavāhana, Ākhaṇḍala, Sahasrākṣa). Many grammatical citations are garbled in transmission and are given as written. "Increase" (འཕེལ་བ), kept as established, is here explicitly glossed vṛtra, so it is the asura Vṛtra. In the Saṅkrandana story, དུས and ཤིན་ཏུ་དུ are read as ངུས / ངུ, "wept," as the etymology requires, and གྱེས as གྱིས. The mountain "Mīna" is presumably Maināka. In the Duścyavana story (Cyavana and the Aśvins, from the Mahābhārata), ཤིང་མདོག, rendered "a share of the fire offering," is literally obscure ("wood-color"); the sense of the Sanskrit story is the Aśvins' share of the soma sacrifice. In the Ahalyā story, འདོམས་ནས, rendered "made himself small," is uncertain. ས་སྲུང ("Earth-Guardian") and "the Well-Inscribed" (ལེགས་བྲིས, Sanskrit lekha) are translated literally. The phrase བ་སུ་ལ་སོ after "sends down rain from the sky" is unintelligible and left untranslated. ཤཱཀྱ་བློ is taken as Śākyabuddhi.  The passage finishes the Amarakośa names of Indra and covers his wife (Paulomī, Śacī, Indrāṇī), his city Amarāvatī, his horse Uccaiḥśravas, his charioteer Mātali, the Nandana grove, the palace Vaijayanta, his son Jayanta, the elephant Airāvata, the vajra and its synonyms (hrādinī, vajra, kuliśa, bhidura, pavi, śatakoṭi, svaru, śamba, dambholi, aśani), vimāna, the divine sages, Sudharmā, amṛta and sudhā, the heavenly Gaṅgā, and Meru. Many grammatical citations are garbled in transmission and are given as written. In "Akṣa means a sense faculty," དབང་པོ is a sense faculty, not Indra; and in "the section on words of various meanings," སྡེ་ཚན is a section of the lexicon, not the name the Host. སློབ་དཔོན་རྒྱལ་བ་ཉིད་མ is read as རྒྱལ་བ་ཉི་མ, Jayāditya (co-author of the Kāśikā). "The Illumination of the Roots" (བྱིངས་རབ་ཏུ་གསལ་བ) is presumably the Dhātupradīpa. དབང་མོ is rendered Indrāṇī here since it names Indra's wife, not the mother-goddess Aindrī. "The Long Essence-Bearer" (བཅུད་ལྡན་རིང་བ), a name of the heavenly river, is translated literally since it cannot be identified. In "deathless beyond measure," ཚ is read as ཚད. The stray syllable ཀནྡྷ in the vimāna gloss is left untranslated.  The passage comments on the Amarakośa lines on Meru, the five divine trees (mandāra, pārijātaka, santāna, kalpavṛkṣa, haricandana), Sanatkumāra, the Aśvins (svarvaidyau, aśvinīsutau, nāsatyau, aśvinau, dasrau, āśvineyau), the apsarases headed by Urvaśī, and the gandharvas (hāhā, hūhū). Many grammatical citations are garbled in transmission and are given as written. Here དབང་ཆེན་མ, earlier rendered Mahendrāṇī as a name of Indra's wife, plainly stands for Urvaśī, the chief apsaras (the text goes on to etymologise urvaśī), so it is rendered Urvaśī. The Sun's wife "She of the Horse" (རྟ་ལྡན་མ) is the mare-formed mother of the Aśvins. Names in the Utpalinī verse and the list of apsarases and gandharvas that cannot be identified with confidence are translated literally ("the Executioner," "Lightning-Radiant," "Fair-Armed," "Chariot of Delight," "Intoxicating," "Well-Adorned," "Muñja-Born," "Plantain-Thighed," "Sweet-Voiced," "Cow-Life," "Vessel-Bearer"). "Says Māna" (ཞེས་པ་མ་ནའོ) and the remark on the single remainder "where grace is not clearly in agreement" are obscure. The Utpalinī is a lexicon; Yaśomitra's "extensive commentary" is his Sphuṭārthā on the Abhidharmakośa.  The passage comments on the Amarakośa names of fire (agni, vaiśvānara, vahni, vītihotra, dhanañjaya, kṛpīṭayoni, jvalana, jātavedas, tanūnapāt, barhis, śuṣman, kṛṣṇavartman, śociṣkeśa, uṣarbudh, āśrayāśa, kṛśānu, pāvaka, anala, rohitāśva, vāyusakha, śikhāvat, āśuśukṣaṇi, hiraṇyaretas, hutabhuj, dahana, havyavāhana, saptārcis, damunas, śukra, citrabhānu, vibhāvasu, śuci, appitta), the submarine fire (aurva, vāḍava), flames (jvāla, kīla, arcis, heti, śikhā), sparks and heat (santāpa, sañjvara). Many grammatical citations and Sanskrit forms are garbled and given as written. The Śālihotra verse is partly unintelligible ("Śānu, Chung-gyé," and the third line); ཀི་མ་ཤུ is read as the red kiṃśuka flower. In "by Umā's curse" (དམོད་སཁཱན་པས) the reading དམོད་པས is followed, and གཞོན་དུ is read as གཞོན་ནུ. མཚུན, rendered "the ancestors" in the Havyavāhana gloss, is uncertain but suits the pair havya/kavya cited next. The seven tongues of flame are identified with the standard list where the Tibetan clearly matches; ལེགས་ཕན ("Well-Beneficial") is left literal, and the inserted words "dahan havyavāhan" are given as written. Ūrva is said to churn his "breast" (བྲང); the usual myth has his thigh.  The passage comments on the Amarakośa names of Yama (dharmarāja, pitṛpati, samavartin, paretarāṭ, kṛtānta, yamunābhrātṛ, śamana, yamarāṭ, yama, kāla, daṇḍadhara, śrāddhadeva, vaivasvata, antaka), of the rākṣasas (rākṣasa, kauṇapa, kravyād, kravyāda, asrapa, āśara, rātriñcara, rātricara, karbura, nikaṣātmaja, yātudhāna, puṇyajana, nairṛta, yātu, rakṣas), of Varuṇa (pracetas, varuṇa, pāśin, yādasāṃpati, appati) and of the wind (śvasana, sparśana, vāyu, mātariśvan, sadāgati, pṛṣadaśva, gandhavaha, gandhavāha, anila, āśuga, samīra, marut). Many grammatical citations are garbled and are given as written. སློབ་དཔོན་རྒྱལ་བ་ཉི་རོ་མ is read as རྒྱལ་བ་ཉི་མ, Jayāditya. In "the Son of the Sun," བི་ཤྭ་ན་ར stands where vivasvat is expected and is given as written. The examples of contrary indication rely on the karaṇa Viṣṭi being called Bhadrā ("the Good") and the planet Mars being called Maṅgala ("the Auspicious"). The stray syllables བྲལ (in the asrapa gloss), ནི་རྀ་ཏྱ་ལ (in the rakṣas gloss), the initial ས before "half a verse," and དྷྲིལ in སྲོག་དྷྲིལ་ཆགས are left untranslated. The Sāmba Purāṇa couplet is partly obscure ("dāe").  The passage comments on the end of the Amarakośa names of wind (samīra, māruta, marut, jagatprāṇa, samīraṇa, nabhasvat, vāta, pavana, pavamāna, prabhañjana), the five bodily winds (prāṇa, apāna, samāna, udāna, vyāna), and the lists of words for speed, swiftness, continuity and intensity, with the rule that these are neuter when they qualify a non-living thing or an action. The Tibetan is badly garbled throughout, especially the grammatical citations, which are given as written. In the verse on the five winds, "purifier, shatterer" and "the full, the purifier" appear to be stray glosses (on pavamāna and prabhañjana) that have entered the verse. Here ཆོས means a property or quality and ཆོས་ཉིད "its very nature," not the doctrinal senses; སྡེ་ཚན is a section of a lexicon; རྡུལ in the list of bodily functions is taken as menses; and སེམས in "the living is complete" (སེམས་ནི་རྫོགས་སོ) appears to stand for sattva, "living being," contrasted in the next sentence with mere properties, though the sentence is obscure. "Vamāna" in the list of the five winds is as written for samāna. The phrase "brgyad bkag" in the gloss on bhṛ is unintelligible and left in transliteration.  The passage comments on the Amarakośa names of Kubera (kubera, tryambakasakha, yakṣarāṭ, guhyakeśvara, manuṣyadharman, dhanada, rājarāja, dhanādhipa, kinnareśa, vaiśravaṇa, paulastya, naravāhana, yakṣa, ekapiṅga, ailavila, śrīda, puṇyajaneśvara), his grove Caitraratha, his son Nalakūbara, Kailāsa, Alakā and the vimāna Puṣpaka, the kinnaras, the nine treasures, and the synonyms of the sky (dyu, div, abhra, vyoman, puṣkara, ambara, nabhas, antarikṣa, gagana, ananta, suravartman, kha, viyat). Many grammatical citations are garbled and are given as written. Here the etymology of Kubera's name is itself the point, so "Bad Body" is given as the meaning while the name is kept as Kubera. "Vast-Light" (འོད་ཡངས) is the sage who is Kubera's grandfather, Pulastya in the usual account, but the Tibetan is translated literally; "Grandson of Vast-Light" corresponds to paulastya. "A man who bore the liṅga" (ལིངྒ་འདོགས་པའི་མི) is uncertain. ཆོད་པ in the yakṣa gloss is read as མཆོད་པ, "worship." Among the nine treasures, the Drum (རྔ་ཅན) answers to mukunda; "the Joyful" and "the Producer" are translated literally. "Bikhāla" after Ratnakośa and the stray syllables ཨཱས, བྲྀལ and ཨཥྐ་རཀྵི are left as written or untranslated.  This passage finishes the names of the sky, then gives the names of direction, the eight elephants of the quarters and their mates, the intermediate directions, the horizon, cloud, thunder and lightning, all with grammatical derivations. Many of the Sanskrit grammatical citations are garbled in the Tibetan; they are given as written, with likely intended forms here.
"Ruka" is taken as the augment tuk, which gives viyat from yam plus kvip.
"Heaven, the Unmoving, the Thoroughly Pervading Viṣṇu" seem to be the lemmas viyat and viṣṇupada; how "the Unmoving" maps onto the Sanskrit is uncertain. The Tibetan for Viṣṇu (khyab 'jug) probably renders viṣṇupada, Viṣṇu's Footstep.
"Ta la ba na" is read as tālavya, the palatal ś.
"Released" (rnam grol) seems to render vihāyas. "Or in the masculine" (Tibetan "or man") renders the Amarakośa's "puṃsi vā"; the All-Illuminated (kun gsal) is ākāśa.
"Half a verse" reads phyed for byed; the following "ca ma" is unintelligible and has been left out.
"From dik and the rest ... kvin" points to the Pāṇinian list ṛtvig, dadhṛk, srag, dik and so on.
The derivation "kena vātena kṣubhyate" belongs to kakubh, the word for a quarter; the Tibetan does not name the lemma.
"Showing of Two" (nyis ston) is unclear and may be corrupt; the derivation that follows (rami, kubi, kāśi with kthan) is for kāṣṭhā.
In "tarati ... jvala, namāṃsi ...", the list of roots is badly garbled; the result is harit, a quarter, glossed as "the Ravisher" from hṛ.
"Vyanakti: hṛ, with ta and iṭ" appears to be a misplaced grammatical fragment inside the list of east, south and west, and is given as written.
Here the Tibetan word for Indra (dbang po) clearly means the god, as lord of the east, and not "sense faculty" as elsewhere.
The elephants of the quarters are identified with their Amarakośa names: Airāvata, Puṇḍarīka, Vāmana, Kumuda, Añjana, Puṣpadanta, Sārvabhauma and Supratīka. Their Tibetan renderings are translated literally. "Earth-Guardian" is the ocean, as earlier in this text. "Ugly-Body" (lus ngan) is Kubera. "Cloud-Holder" for the first she-elephant renders Abhramu.
"Non-direction", "neuter, indeclinable" renders the Amarakośa's "klībāvyayaṃ tv apadiśam". "Three" in "those three lie at the junction" is probably a copyist slip and is not translated.
Under cloud:
- The Tibetan writes "as" (as) for ap, the recurring ས-for-པ slip.
- "Master Supreme Person" is Puruṣottamadeva, author of the Bhāṣāvṛtti.
- Two different lemmas are both rendered "Water-Pourer" in Tibetan (chu 'bebs): megha, and then vārivāha. "Water's Mount" is balāhaka. The Tibetan word chu 'dzin renders both abhra (Water-Holder) and ambubhṛt (Water-Bearer).
- "Stan yi nu jhra ya" is read as a rule ending in "jhayaḥ", which accounts for the v of taḍitvat.
- "For 'it destroys torment'" is the derivation of ghana, from han by "mūrtau ghanaḥ".
- "Ki ca" is kirac (mudira).
- The sentence on jīva and mūtrayati is partly garbled, and "dīmvuta" is read as jīmūta.
- "Mud-dga'-ba" is left as written. It may refer to the Meghadūta, which names smoke, light, water and wind as the components of a cloud.
- "Heron-Abode" likely plays on balākā, crane, to explain balāhaka.
- "Ja la mug" in "cloud garland" is unintelligible and is omitted.
Under thunder and lightning:
- "Fierce Sound" renders sgra gcan, which usually names Rāhu but here closes a list of names of thunder.
- The Resounder is kept as established, though here it is a name of thunder rather than of the cloud.
- "Happiness-Drinker" is śampā; the suffix given as "ki" is unclear.
- "Hundred-Sounding" and "Sound-Scatterer" render śatahradā and hrādinī.
- "In the remaining senses" renders the rule "śeṣe".
- "The Instant" renders kṣaṇaprabhā.
- "Because it strikes" gives taḍit; the later "shines and flickers" gives vidyut.
- "White Twice-Drinker" is given as written.
- Saudāmanī is "the Brilliant". The "one part of it" is probably the mountain Sudāman, which the Tibetan does not name.
- The Rabhasa is the lexicon Rabhasakośa.
- The Mover and the Restless are cañcalā and capalā, with alac.  This passage continues the Amarakośa's section on the heavens. It covers thunder and the thunderbolt, the rainbow, rain, drought, downpour, spray, hail, overcast weather, concealment, the moon and its parts, moonlight, the moon's mark, beauty, frost and snow, and coolness.
The Tibetan for "sky-iron" (gnam lcags) renders aśani, the thunderbolt.
The phrase that follows "clashing with one another" (kyu ta la ngo) is unintelligible and has been passed over.
"Though it is fire": the word here (me) means fire, not the god Agni.
"Straight" renders the Amarakośa's note that the straight rainbow is called rohita. The Tibetan "dge dgu" is read as the word for "bends".
"Shower" renders zhod. "Alike" renders the Amarakośa's "samau", said of the two words for drought, avagraha and avagrāha.
"Heavy downpour": the word drag po here means "violent", not the god Rudra.
"Kṣipaka and the rest" refers to the exception that keeps karakā from becoming karikā.
"Bad day" reads nyin ngan for the Tibetan yul ngan, "bad place", since the lemma is durdina.
The derivations of candra, candramas, indu, jaivātṛka, soma and glau are given as the Tibetan has them; several of the citations of rules are garbled. In particular, "dūra and the rest", "pra jā du ra" (read as the sphāyi list), "ang it" and "yuka" stand for Uṇādi rules that cannot be restored with certainty.
The Ocean-Born here is the moon, born from the churning of the ocean. It is not the elephant Airāvata, who had the same epithet in the previous passage.
"Pressing out" renders the Tibetan "manifest birth", the usual Tibetan rendering of abhiṣava, the pressing of soma.
A stray syllable "glaḥ" before "vow" in the passage on the twice-born is taken as a copyist's repetition of glau and is not translated.
"Hare-Bearer" and "Deer-Marked" refer to the Śaśa Jātaka, in which the bodhisattva as a hare offered his own body and was set in the moon.
"Mark on the moon" renders mtshan nyid, here the Amarakośa's lemma for the moon's spot rather than a philosophical "characteristic". It is translated as "characteristic" only for lakṣaṇa in the list of synonyms.
"Kapiri and the rest" is the Pāṇinian class kapilaka and the rest, in which l and r alternate.
Sukhama is read as suṣamā, and śvabhā as śobhā.
The words for frost and snow (avaśyāya, nīhāra, tuṣāra, tuhina, hima, prāleya, mahikā) follow the Amarakośa.
"Its short vowel changes" follows the Tibetan, which says "short"; the rule meant actually lengthens the prefix ni to nī.
"Himaṃ prālaya" is read as hima and prāleya.
"Object of touch" reads reg bya for rig bya.  This passage finishes cool things. It then covers the Pole Star, Canopus (Agastya) and his wife Lopāmudrā, general words for stars, several lunar mansions (Aśvinī, Viśākhā, Puṣya, Dhaniṣṭhā, Bhādrapadā, Mṛgaśiras), the planets Jupiter, Venus, Mars, Mercury and Saturn, Rāhu, the seven sages, a few zodiac terms, and the first names of the sun.
"Chill gust" reads the Tibetan lhags ngar as lhags pa, a cold wind.
"By the sūtra": the Tibetan has mdos, read here as mdo yis, "by the sūtra". It is not the ritual thread-cross. The rule cited, "from many," is uncertain.
"Royal sage" renders drang srong gi rgyal po, for rājarṣi.
The Unmoving (aga) is a mountain, as earlier. "Pāraskara and the rest" is the class that takes the augment s, giving Agastya.
In the Agastya story, "Varuṇa" renders chu lha, which here names one of the two sages whose seed produced Agastya. It is translated with the house rendering for the god of the waters.
"Ata iñ" is the Pāṇinian rule for the patronymic iñ; the usual compound form is Maitrāvaruṇi.
Sadharmiṇī normally means lawful wife. The Tibetan explains chos, "dharma," as the quality of pride, so "quality" is used rather than "Dharma".
"Seal" renders phyag rgya, mudrā, explaining the second half of Lopāmudrā.
Uḍḍupa is given as written; uḍu is the usual word for star.
"The Clear One" (gsal ba) is the Tibetan rendering of Dakṣa.
The Tibetan names of the lunar mansions are rendered by their Sanskrit equivalents:
- tha skar is Aśvinī
- sa ga is Viśākhā
- rgyal is Puṣya
- mon gre is Dhaniṣṭhā
- khrums is Bhādrapadā
- mgo is Mṛgaśiras
- snar ma is Rohiṇī
Śraviṣṭhā and "samādhaniṣṭha" are partly garbled in the Tibetan and given as written. "Kyap" is the suffix kyap.
"Lord of the planets" in the explanation of bhadrapada is unclear and is given as written.
"Wounded" reads bcom for the Tibetan bcos.
In this text Aṅgiras is rendered "the Sun" (nyi ma), so Āṅgirasa is "Lineage of the Sun". The "Variegated-Crested" (Citraśikhaṇḍin) are the seven sages.
The planets are given by their usual Tibetan names: gza' pa sangs is Venus, gza' bkra shis is Mars (Maṅgala, "the Auspicious"), gza' lhag is Mercury, and gza' spen pa is Saturn.
"Evil-Abandoner" (ngan spong) is the Tibetan rendering of Bhṛgu.
"Red-Eye" for aṅgāraka follows the Tibetan; the Sanskrit means "ember".
"Illuminator of Heaven" (mtho ris gsal byed) renders svarbhānu. Here gsal byed is not the elephant Añjana, for whom the same Tibetan word was used earlier.
Hiraṇyakaśipu is called a sage in the Tibetan, though he is usually a daitya; the Tibetan names him "'drob skyong gser can".
Pulastya keeps the established name Vast-Light.
"Sāmba's Ancient Account" (sam ba'i sngon rabs) is probably the Sāmbapurāṇa.
"Born of Aditi" renders mi sbyin skyes, in which mi sbyin, "not giving," is the Tibetan rendering of Aditi.  This passage gives the remaining thirty-seven names of the sun, the sun's attendants and charioteer, the halo, rays, light, warmth and mirage, time, the lunar day and the day.
Several Tibetan words here have been rendered differently from earlier passages, because the context requires it:
- gsal byed: earlier the elephant Añjana; here "Illuminator", once in prabhākara and once for bhānu.
- gsal ba: earlier Dakṣa; here simply "clarity".
- 'od zer can: earlier Marīci; here "the Radiant" for savitṛ.
- 'byung po: here "beings", not a class of spirits.
- dkyil 'khor: here the solar halo, rendered "maṇḍala, the circle".
- mdos: in "by the sūtra beginning with vṛ", read as mdo yis, "by the sūtra".
The verse on the seven horses of the sun is quoted from a commentary on prosody (sdeb sbyor). "The wind" there keeps the established rendering. The craftsman of the gods (lha'i bzo bo) and the All-Maker (las thams cad pa) are Viśvakarman, who trimmed the sun's body; this explains vikartana. A Tibetan sentence has slipped in between the epithet and the verse.
The bracketed sentence after pūṣan seems to be an interpolated translators' note on mārtaṇḍa.
- It says that Drokmi ('brog mi), Düle Gyal (bdud las rgyal, "Victor over Māra", taken as a personal name) and Lo Drakpa (lo grags pa, probably "Lotsāwa Drakpa") rendered the word "Thick Womb" (mngal stug).
- It says that Sapaṇ (sa paṇ, probably Sakya Paṇḍita) rendered it "Unrounded" (ma zlum).
- It ends that the commentary does not make the reason clear.
- "Destroyed by Rain" (char gyis 'jig) heads the note and is unclear.
- These identifications are tentative, and the rendering is literal. The sentence "because it nourishes the bodily support" belongs to pūṣan.
"Lord of the Planets ... the gods' guru" refers to Jupiter.
The sun's attendants (Māṭhara, Piṅgala, Daṇḍa) follow the Amarakośa. Here Indra appears "in the form of the Staff" (Daṇḍa) and Agni as the reddish-yellow (Piṅgala). Māṭhara is identified with Yama, and the Tibetan "abider" (gnas byed) seems to gloss Māṭhara.
"Vinatā" renders 'dul byed ma; her story was told earlier in the text.
"Kyu" reads the Tibetan "kya ba".
"Ik in place of yaṇ" is the vowel substitution (samprasāraṇa) of u for v.
"Sickness" (nad) for am, and "reviling" (spyo ba) for bhas, follow the root lists, in which am means going and sickness, and bhas means reviling and shining.
"Presence" (dngos po) for chavi follows the Tibetan. "Shining lamp" for śocis is literal.
The Tibetan "stod" in "thousand-rayed" is read as stong, "thousand".
"Darkness-Cooled" (mun bsil) for ghasra is literal.  This passage covers daybreak and evening, the full moon and new moon, the units of time, the courses of the sun, the twelve months with their Amarakośa synonyms, the six seasons, the year, and the cosmic ages.
"Prabhāta is evening" follows the Tibetan (dgongs mo), though prabhāta normally means morning; the sentence may be corrupt.
"Saparvan ... sandhi ... pratipad" renders the Amarakośa line on the juncture between the fifteenth and the first lunar day.
"Night-Maker" here renders niśākara, the moon; the established Tibetan is kept.
A stray syllable "sde" in the line on the blink is left untranslated.

The term rendered "pretas" (yi dgas) stands here for the pitṛs, the departed ancestors, whose day is a human month.
"Three hundred and sixty" corrects the Tibetan "three hundred and sixteen" (bcu drug for drug cu). The total of twelve thousand divine years must equal four million three hundred and twenty thousand human years, which agrees with the four ages listed at the end.
"Brahmā's pair" means his day and night.

The Ādibuddha verse uses word-numerals read from the lowest place upward; "arm-goer" stands for the serpent (bhujaga), eight, which the following key does not list. The key gives "teeth" as thirty-two although teeth does not occur in the verses as transmitted, while "time" in the summary verse must stand for three. The figures are given as the Tibetan writes them: the Tretā appears as 1,276,000 (the verse and the preceding passage give 1,296,000) and the sum of the four ages as 432,000 (the stated total is 4,320,000). Likewise "seventy-two" ages for a Manu conflicts with both the 284 human ages given and the Sūryasiddhānta's seventy-one; "one hundred and ninety-four" divine ages should be nine hundred and ninety-four to agree with the stated 3,976 human ages; and the Sūryasiddhānta quotation reads "twelve" Manus in a kalpa where the original has fourteen. In the opening verse the Tibetan names the first tone "earth-holder," an alternative rendering of gāndhāra (etymologized as "holding the earth"); classical theory likewise assigns gāndhāra and niṣāda to compassion. The word rendered "able" in the pañcāsya entry is the word otherwise meaning patience, here used in the sense of being capable. The explanation of haryakṣa rests on a double sense of hari, which means both "seizer" and "tawny." The Tibetan here uses the word dred, which usually means bear, but the description of a dog-shaped animal the size of a large dog that preys on livestock and people, and the Sanskrit tarakṣu, point to the hyena. The following is a note about this translation.

This is an AI-generated translation from the AI Stupa project. It was produced by a careful, doctrine-aware process aiming at a high degree of accuracy, but it has not been reviewed by a human translator and may contain errors. It is offered as a provisional translation for those texts of the Tibetan Buddhist canon that have not yet received an authoritative human translation. Where a published translation exists — above all from 84000: Translating the Words of the Buddha (84000.co) — that translation is definitive and should be preferred; please consult it instead. These translations are released into the public domain (CC0) in the spirit of spreading the Dharma to all corners of the earth.

Just as the Bodhi tree now standing at Bodh Gayā neither is nor is not the same tree beneath which the Buddha attained awakening, so too this stupa is intended as the next generation of stupa — dependently arising according to the conditions of its environment, and ceaselessly proclaiming the Dharma for the benefit of all sentient beings.

May whoever encounters these words derive great benefit.
